Notes on applicative matching logic
Laurenţiu Leuştean, [2mm] Institute for Logic, Data Science, Bucharest, [1mm] LOS, Faculty of Mathematics, Computer Science, University of Bucharest, [1mm] Simion Stoilow Institute of Mathematics of the Romanian Academy

Abstract




1
Introduction

Matching logic was introduced by Grigore Roşu
[
8
,
7
]
.

and collaborators
as a logic for defining the formal semantics of programming languages and for specifying
and reasoning about the behavior of programs. Applicative Matching Logic (AML), a functional variant of ML,
was introduced recently
[
3
]
and developed further in
[
5
,
2
]

These lecture notes present basic definitions and results on AML, with very rigorous and extremely
detailed proofs. They can be used as an introductory text in the theory of AML. We point out that the definition of
patterns in these notes is more general than the one from
[
3
]
, as we do not require the positivity of
the pattern in the definition of the
\mu
binder.

Monk’s textbook on mathematical logic
[
6
]
has an enormous influence on the notes. A number of notations and results for first-order logic from Monk’s book are adapted to AML.
The set-theoretic notions and properties used in the notes are given in Appendix
B
.

2
Language

Definition 2.1
.

An
applicative matching logic (AML) signature
(or simply
signature
) is a triple

\tau=(EVar,SVar,\Sigma)
,
where

(i)

EVar=\{v_{n}\mid n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
is a countable set of
element variables
.

(ii)

SVar=\{V_{n}\mid n\in{\mathbb{N}}\}
is a countable set of
set variables
.

(iii)

\Sigma
is a set of
constants
.

The sets
EVar
,
SVar
and
\Sigma
are pairwise disjoint.

We denote element variables by
x,y,z,x_{1},x_{2},\ldots
, set variables by
X,Y,Z,X_{1},X_{2},\ldots

and symbols by
\sigma,f,g,\ldots
.

In the sequel,
\tau=(EVar,SVar,\Sigma)
is a signature.

Definition 2.2
.

The set
Sym_{\tau}
of
\tau
-symbols
is defined as

Sym_{\tau}=EVar\cup SVar\cup\{\to,\exists,\mu,Appl\}\cup\Sigma.

Definition 2.3
.

The set
Expr_{\tau}
of
\tau
-expressions
is the set of all expressions
over
Sym_{\tau}
.

Definition 2.4
.

An
atomic
\tau
-pattern
is an element variable, a set variable or a constant.
We shall use the notation
AtomicPattern_{\tau}
for the set of atomic
\tau
-patterns.

Definition 2.5
.

The
\tau
-patterns
are the
\tau
-expressions inductively defined as follows:

(i)

Every atomic
\tau
-pattern is a
\tau
-pattern.

(ii)

If
\varphi
and
\psi
are
\tau
-patterns, then
Appl\varphi\psi
is a
\tau
-pattern.

(iii)

If
\varphi
and
\psi
are
\tau
-patterns, then
\to\varphi\psi
is a
\tau
-pattern.

(iv)

If
\varphi
is a
\tau
-pattern and
x
is an element variable, then
\exists x\varphi
is a
\tau
-pattern.

(v)

If
\varphi
is a
\tau
-pattern and
X
is a set variable, then
\mu X\varphi
is a
\tau
-pattern.

(vi)

Only the expressions obtained by applying the above rules are
\tau
-patterns.

We use the Polish notation in the definition of
\tau
-patterns as this notation allows us
to obtain the unique readability of
\tau
-patterns (see Proposition
2.9
),
a fundamental property.

The set of
\tau
-patterns is denoted by
Pattern_{\tau}
and
\tau
-patterns are denoted
by
\varphi,\psi,\chi,\ldots
.

For any
\tau
-pattern
\varphi
, we denote by
EVar(\varphi)

the set of element variables occuring in
\varphi
and by
SVar(\varphi)
the set
of set variables occuring in
\varphi
.

Remark 2.6
.

The definition of
\tau
-patterns can be written using the BNF notation:

\displaystyle\varphi::=

\displaystyle x\in EVar\mid X\in SVar\mid\sigma\in\Sigma\mid Appl\varphi\varphi\mid\to\varphi\varphi

\displaystyle\mid\exists x\varphi\text{~if~}x\in EVar

\displaystyle\mid\mu X\varphi\text{~if~}x\in SVar.

Definition 2.7

(Alternative definition for
\tau
-patterns)
.

The set of
\tau
-patterns
is the intersection of all
sets
\Gamma
of
\tau
-expressions that have the following properties:

(i)

\Gamma
contains all atomic
\tau
-patterns.

(ii)

\Gamma
is closed to
Appl
,
\to
,
\exists x
(for any element variable
x
),
and
\mu X
(for any set variable
X
), that is:

if
\varphi,\psi\in\Gamma
, then
Appl\varphi\psi
,
\to\varphi\psi
,
\exists x\varphi
,
\mu X\varphi\in\Gamma
.

When the signature
\tau
is clear from the context, we shall write simply expression(s), pattern(s)
and we shall denote the set of expressions by
Expr
, the set of
patterns by
Pattern
, the set of atomic patterns by
AtomicPattern
, etc..

Proposition 2.8

(Induction principle on patterns)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a set of patterns satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\Gamma
contains all atomic patterns.

(ii)

\Gamma
is closed to
Appl
,
\to
,
\exists x
(for any element variable
x
), and
\mu X
(for any set variable
X
).

Then
\Gamma=Pattern
.

Proof.

By hypothesis,
\Gamma\subseteq Pattern
. By Definition
2.7
, we get that

Pattern\subseteq\Gamma
.
∎

Induction principle on patterns is used to prove that all patterns have a property
\mathcal{P}
: we define

\Gamma
as the set of all patterns satisfying
\mathcal{P}
and apply
induction on patterns to obtain that
\Gamma=Pattern
.

2.1
Unique readability

Proposition 2.9

(Unique readability of patterns)
.

(i)

Any pattern has a positive length.

(ii)

If
\varphi
is a pattern, then one of the following hold:

(a)

\varphi=x
, where
x\in EVar
.

(b)

\varphi=X
, where
X\in SVar
.

(c)

\varphi=\sigma
, where
\sigma\in\Sigma
.

(d)

\varphi=Appl\psi\chi
, where
\psi,\chi
are patterns.

(e)

\varphi=\to\psi\chi
, where
\psi,\chi
are patterns.

(f)

\varphi=\exists x\psi
, where
x
is an element variable and
\psi
is a pattern.

(g)

\varphi=\mu X\psi
, where
X
is a set variable and
\psi
is a pattern.

(iii)

Any proper initial segment of a pattern is not a pattern.

(iv)

If
\varphi
is a pattern, then exactly one of the cases from (
ii
)
holds. Moreover,
\varphi
can be written in a unique way in one of these forms.

Proof.

(i)

Let
\Gamma
be the set of patterns of positive length. We prove that
\Gamma=Pattern
using
the Induction principle on patterns (Proposition
2.8
).

(a)

If
\varphi
is an atomic pattern, then its length is
1
, so
\varphi\in\Gamma
.

(b)

If
\varphi,\psi\in\Gamma
, hence they have positive length, then obviously
the patterns
Appl\varphi\psi
,
\to\varphi\psi
,
\exists x\varphi
,
\mu X\varphi\in\Gamma
have positive
length, hence they are in
\Gamma
.

(ii)

Let
\Gamma_{1}=\{Appl\psi\chi\mid\psi,\chi\in Pattern\}
,
\Gamma_{2}=\{\to\psi\chi\mid\psi,\chi\in Pattern\}
,

\Gamma_{3}=\{\exists x\psi\mid x\in EVar\text{~ and~}\psi\in Pattern\}
and

\Gamma_{4}=\{\mu X\psi\mid X\in SVar\text{~ and~}\psi\in Pattern\}
.
Define

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle=EVar\cup SVar\cup\Sigma\cup\Gamma_{1}\cup\Gamma_{2}\cup\Gamma_{3}\cup\Gamma_{4}.

Then obviously,
\Gamma\subseteq Pattern
. We prove that
\Gamma=Pattern
using the
Induction principle on patterns (Proposition
2.8
).

(a)

As
EVar\cup SVar\cup\Sigma\subseteq\Gamma
, we have that
\Gamma
contains all atomic patterns.

(b)

Let
\varphi,\psi\in\Gamma
,
x\in EVar
and
X\in SVar
. Then

Appl\varphi\psi\in\Gamma_{1}\subseteq\Gamma
,
\to\varphi\psi\in\Gamma_{2}\subseteq\Gamma
,
\exists x\varphi\in\Gamma_{3}\subseteq\Gamma
, and

\mu X\varphi\in\Gamma_{4}\subseteq\Gamma
. Thus,
\Gamma
is closed to
Appl
,
\to
,
\exists x
, and
\mu X
.

(iii)

As, by (
i
), patterns have positive length, it follows that we have to prove that for all
n\geq 1
,

(P)

if
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
is a pattern of length
n
, then for any
0\leq i<n-1
,

\varphi=\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{i}
is not a pattern.

The proof is by induction on
n
.

n=1
: Then one cannot have
0\leq i<0
, hence (P) holds.

Assume that
n>1
and that (P) holds for any pattern of length
<n
. Let
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
be a pattern of length
n
.
By (
ii
), we have the following cases:

(a)

\varphi=\star\psi\chi
, where
\star\in\{Appl,\to\}
and
\psi
,
\chi
are patterns. Thus,
\varphi_{0}=\star
,

\psi=\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{k-1}
and

\chi=\varphi_{k}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
, where
2\leq k\leq n-1
.

Let
0\leq i<n-1
and assume, by contradiction, that
\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{i}
is a pattern.
Applying again (
ii
), it follows that
\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{i}=\star\psi^{1}\chi^{1}
, where
\psi^{1}
,
\chi^{1}
are patterns.
Thus,
\psi^{1}=\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{p-1}
,
\chi^{1}=\varphi_{p}\ldots\varphi_{i}
, where
2\leq p\leq i
. We have the following cases:

(1)

p<k
. Then
\psi^{1}
is a proper initial segment of
\psi
. As the length of
\psi
is
<n
, we can apply
the induction hypothesis to get that
\psi^{1}
is not a pattern. We have obtained a contradiction.

(2)

p=k
. Then
\psi^{1}=\psi
and
\chi^{1}
is a proper initial segment of
\chi
. As the length of
\chi
is
<n
, we can apply
the induction hypothesis to get that
\chi^{1}
is not a pattern. We have obtained a contradiction.

(3)

p>k
. Then
\psi
is a proper initial segment of
\psi^{1}
. As the length of
\psi^{1}
is
<n
, we can apply
the induction hypothesis to get that
\psi
is not a pattern. We have obtained a contradiction.

(b)

\varphi=\theta\psi
, where
\theta\in\{\exists x,\mu X\}
for some
x\in EVar
and
X\in SVar
and
\psi
is a pattern.
Then
\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}=\theta
and
\psi=\varphi_{2}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
. Let
0\leq i<n-1
and assume, by contradiction,
that
\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{i}
is a pattern. Applying again (
ii
), it follows that
\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{i}=\theta\psi^{1}
,
where
\psi^{1}=\varphi_{2}\ldots\varphi_{i}
is a pattern. Then
\psi^{1}
is a proper initial segment of
\psi
.
As the length of
\psi
is
<n
, we can apply the induction hypothesis to get that
\psi^{1}
is
not a pattern. We have obtained a contradiction.

(iv)

is an immediate consequece of (
ii
) and (
iii
).

∎

The following results will be useful in Subsections
2.4
and
2.5
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
be a pattern and suppose that
\varphi_{i}\in\{\exists,\mu\}
for some
i=0,\ldots,n-1
.
Then there exists a unique
j
such that
i<j\leq n-1
and

\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is a pattern.

Proof.

Let us prove first the uniqueness. Assume, by contradiction, that

i<j<k\leq n-1
are such that
\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
and
\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{k}
are both patterns. As
\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is a
proper initial segment of
\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{k}
, it follows, by Proposition
2.9
.(
iii
)
that
\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is not a pattern. We have obtained a contradiction.

Let us prove in the sequel the existence.

As, by Proposition
2.9
.(
i
), patterns have positive length, it follows that we have to prove that for all
n\geq 1
,

(P)

if
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
is a pattern of length
n
and
\varphi_{i}\in\{\exists,\mu\}
for some
i=0,\ldots,n-1
,

then there exists
j
such that
i<j\leq n-1
and

\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is a pattern.

The proof is by induction on
n
.

n=1
. Then
\varphi=\varphi_{0}
is an atomic pattern, so there exists no
i
satisfying the premise in (P), hence (P) holds.

Assume that
n>1
and that (P) holds for any pattern of length
<n
. Let
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
be a pattern of length
n

such that
\varphi_{i}\in\{\exists,\mu\}
for some
i=0,\ldots,n-1
.
By Proposition
2.9
.(
ii
), we have the following cases:

(i)

\varphi=\circ\psi\chi
, where
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
and
\psi
,
\chi
are patterns.
Thus,
\varphi_{0}=\circ
,

\psi=\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{k-1}
and

\chi=\varphi_{k}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
, where
2\leq k\leq n-1
. We have the following cases:

(a)

i\leq k-1
. Then
\varphi_{i}
occurs in
\psi
. As the length of
\psi
is
<n
, we can apply
the induction hypothesis to get the existence of
j
such that
i<j\leq k-1
and
\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is a pattern.

(b)

i\geq k
. Then
\varphi_{i}
occurs in
\chi
. As the length of
\chi
is
<n
, we can apply
the induction hypothesis to get the existence of
j
such that
i<j\leq n-1
and
\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is a pattern.

(ii)

\varphi=\theta\psi
, where
\theta\in\{\exists x,\mu X\}
for some
x\in EVar
or
X\in SVar
and
\psi
is a pattern.
Then
\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}=\theta
and
\psi=\varphi_{2}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
. We have the following cases:

(a)

i=0
. Then
j=n-1
and
\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}=\varphi
is a pattern.

(b)

2\leq i\leq n-1
. Then
\varphi_{i}
occurs in
\psi
. As the length of
\psi
is
<n
, we can apply
the induction hypothesis to get the existence of
j
such that
i<j\leq n-1
and
\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is a pattern.

∎

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
be a pattern and suppose that
\varphi_{i}\in\{Appl,\to\}
for some
i=0,\ldots,n-1
.
Then there exist unique
j
,
l
such that
i<j<l\leq n-1
and

\varphi_{i+1}\ldots\varphi_{j}
,
\varphi_{j+1}\ldots\varphi_{l}
are patterns.

Proof.

Let us prove first the uniqueness. Assume, by contradiction, that
i<j<l\leq n-1
and
i<j_{1}<l_{1}\leq n-1
are such that

\psi=\varphi_{i+1}\ldots\varphi_{j}
,
\chi=\varphi_{j+1}\ldots\varphi_{l}
,
\psi^{1}=\varphi_{i+1}\ldots\varphi_{j_{1}}
,

\chi=\varphi_{j+1}\ldots\varphi_{l_{1}}
are patterns. If
j\neq j_{1}
, then either
j<j_{1}
or
j_{1}<j
, hence one of

\psi
,
\psi^{1}
is a proper initial segment of the other one. By
Proposition
2.9
.(
iii
), we get that one of

\psi
,
\psi^{1}
is not a pattern. We have obtained a contradiction. Thus, we must have
j=j_{1}
.
We prove similarly that we must have
l=l_{1}
.

Let us prove in the sequel the existence.

As, by Proposition
2.9
.(
i
), patterns have positive length,
we have to prove that for all
n\geq 1
,

(P)

if
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
is a pattern of length
n
and
\varphi_{i}\in\{Appl,\to\}
for some
i=0,\ldots,n-1
,

then there exist
j
,
l
such that
i<j<l\leq n-1
and

\varphi_{i+1}\ldots\varphi_{j}
,
\varphi_{j+1}\ldots\varphi_{l}
are patterns.

The proof is by induction on
n
.

n=1
. Then
\varphi=\varphi_{0}
is an atomic pattern, so there exists no
i
satisfying the premise in (P),
so (P) holds.

Assume that
n>1
and that (P) holds for any pattern of length
<n
. Let
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
be a pattern of length
n

such that
\varphi_{i}\in\{Appl,\to\}
for some
i=0,\ldots,n-1
.
By Proposition
2.9
.(
ii
), we have the following cases:

(i)

\varphi=\circ\psi\chi
, where
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
and
\psi
,
\chi
are patterns.
Thus,
\varphi_{0}=\circ
,

\psi=\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{k-1}
and

\chi=\varphi_{k}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
, where
2\leq k\leq n-1
. We have the following cases:

(a)

i=0
. Then we can take
j=k-1
and
l=n-1
.

(b)

i\in\{1,\ldots,k-1\}
. Then
\varphi_{i}
occurs in
\psi
. As the length of
\psi
is
<n
, we can apply
the induction hypothesis to get the conclusion.

(c)

i\geq k
. Then
\varphi_{i}
occurs in
\chi
. As the length of
\chi
is
<n
, we can apply
the induction hypothesis to get the conclusion

(ii)

\varphi=\theta\psi
, where
\theta\in\{\exists x,\mu X\}
for some
x\in EVar
and
X\in SVar
and
\psi
is a pattern.
Then
\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}=\theta
and
\psi=\varphi_{2}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
. As
\varphi_{i}
does not occur in
\theta
, it follows that

\varphi_{i}
occurs in
\psi
. As the length of
\psi
is
<n
, we can apply
the induction hypothesis to get the conclusion.

∎

2.2
Recursion principle on patterns

Proposition 2.12

(Recursion principle on patterns)
.

Let
D
be a set and the mappings

\displaystyle G_{0}:AtomicPattern\to D,

\displaystyle\quad G_{Appl},\,G_{\to}:D^{2}\times Pattern^{2}\to D,

(1)

\displaystyle G_{\exists}:D\times EVar\times Pattern\to D,

\displaystyle\quad G_{\mu}:D\times SVar\times Pattern\to D.

(2)

Then there exists a unique mapping

F:Pattern\to D

that satisfies the following properties:

(i)

F(\varphi)=G_{0}(\varphi)
for any atomic pattern
\varphi
.

(ii)

F(Appl\varphi\psi)=G_{Appl}(F(\varphi),F(\psi),\varphi,\psi)
for any patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
.

(iii)

F(\to\varphi\psi)=G_{\to}(F(\varphi),F(\psi),\varphi,\psi)
for any patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
.

(iv)

F(\exists x\varphi)=G_{\exists}(F(\varphi),x,\varphi)
for any pattern
\varphi
and any element variable
x
.

(v)

F(\mu X\varphi)=G_{\mu}(F(\varphi),X,\varphi)
for any pattern
\varphi
and any set variable
X
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
2.9
.
∎

2.3
Subpatterns

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern. A subpattern of
\varphi
is a pattern
\psi
that occurs in
\varphi
.

Notation 2.14
.

We denote by
SubPattern(\varphi)
the set of subpatterns of
\varphi
.

Remark 2.15

(Alternative definition)
.

The mapping

SubPattern:Pattern\to 2^{Pattern},\quad\varphi\mapsto SubPattern(\varphi)

can be defined by recursion on patterns as follows:

SubPattern(\varphi)

=

\{\varphi\}
if
\varphi
is an atomic pattern,

SubPattern(Appl\varphi\psi)

=

SubPattern(\varphi)\cup SubPattern(\psi)\cup\{Appl\varphi\psi\}
,

SubPattern(\to\varphi\psi)

=

SubPattern(\varphi)\cup SubPattern(\psi)\cup\{\to\varphi\psi\}
,

SubPattern(\exists x\varphi)

=

SubPattern(\varphi)\cup\{\exists x\varphi\}
,

SubPattern(\mu X\varphi)

=

SubPattern(\varphi)\cup\{\mu X\varphi\}
.

Proof.

Apply Recursion principle on patterns (Proposition
2.12
) with

D=2^{Pattern}
and

\displaystyle G_{0}(\varphi)=\{\varphi\},

\displaystyle\quad G_{\circ}(\Gamma,\Delta,\varphi,\psi)=\Gamma\cup\Delta\cup\{\circ\varphi\psi\}\text{~for~}\circ\in\{Appl,\to\},

\displaystyle G_{\exists}(\Gamma,x,\varphi)=\Gamma\cup\{\exists x\varphi\}

\displaystyle\quad G_{\mu}(\Gamma,X,\varphi)=\Gamma\cup\{\mu X\varphi\}.

Then

(i)

SubPattern(\varphi)=\{\varphi\}=G_{0}(\varphi)
if
\varphi
is an atomic pattern.

(ii)

For
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
, we have that

\displaystyle SubPattern(\circ\varphi\psi)

\displaystyle=SubPattern(\varphi)\cup SubPattern(\psi)\cup\{\circ\varphi\psi\}

\displaystyle=G_{\circ}(SubPattern(\varphi),SubPattern(\psi),\varphi,\psi).

(iii)

SubPattern(\exists x\varphi)=SubPattern(\varphi)\cup\{\exists x\varphi\}=G_{\exists}(SubPattern(\varphi),x,\varphi)
.

(iv)

SubPattern(\mu X\varphi)=SubPattern(\varphi)\cup\{\mu X\varphi\}=G_{\mu}(SubPattern(\varphi),X,\varphi)
.

Thus,
SubPattern:Pattern\to 2^{Pattern}
is the unique mapping given by Proposition
2.12
.
∎

2.4
Free and bound variables

Definition 2.16
.

Let
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
be a pattern and
x
be an element variable.

(i)

We say that
\exists
is
a quantifier on
x
at the
i
th place with scope
\psi
if

\varphi_{i}=\exists
,
\varphi_{i+1}=x
and
\psi=\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is the unique pattern given by Proposition
2.10
.

(ii)

We say that
x

occurs bound at the
k
th place of
\varphi
if

\varphi_{k}=x
and
there exist
0\leq i,j\leq n-1
such that
i<k\leq j
and

\exists
is a quantifier on
x
at the
i
th place with scope
\psi=\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
.

(iii)

If
\varphi_{k}=x
but
x
does not occur bound at the
k
th place of
\varphi
, we say that

x

occurs free at the
k
th place of
\varphi
.

(iv)

x
is a
bound variable
of
\varphi
if there exists

k
such that
x
occurs bound at the
k
th place of
\varphi
.

(v)

x
is a
free variable
of
\varphi
if there exists
k
such that

x
occurs free at the
k
th place of
\varphi
.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
be a pattern and
X
be a set variable.

(i)

We say that
\mu
is
a binder on
X
at the
i
th place with scope
\psi
if

\varphi_{i}=\mu
,
\varphi_{i+1}=X
and
\psi=\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is the unique pattern given
by Proposition
2.10
.

(ii)

We say that
X

occurs bound at the
k
th place of
\varphi
if

\varphi_{k}=X
and there exist
0\leq i,j\leq n-1
such that
i<k\leq j
and

\mu
is a binder on
X
at the
i
th place with scope
\psi=\varphi_{i}\ldots\varphi_{j}
.

(iii)

If
\varphi_{k}=X
but
X
does not occur bound at the
k
th place of
\varphi
, we say that

X

occurs free at the
k
th place of
\varphi
.

(iv)

X
is a
bound variable
of
\varphi
if there exists

k
such that
X
occurs bound at the
k
th place of
\varphi
.

(v)

X
is a
free variable
of
\varphi
if there exists
k
such that

X
occurs free at the
k
th place of
\varphi
.

Notation 2.18
.

FV(\varphi)=
the set of free element and set variables of
\varphi
.

Remark 2.19

(Alternative definition)
.

The mapping

FV:Pattern\to 2^{EVar\cup SVar},\quad\varphi\mapsto FV(\varphi)

can be defined by recursion on patterns as follows:

FV(\varphi)

=

EVar(\varphi)\cup SVar(\varphi)

if
\varphi
is an atomic pattern,

FV(\circ\varphi\psi)

=

FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi)

for
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
,

FV(\exists x\varphi)

=

FV(\varphi)\setminus\{x\}
,

FV(\mu X\varphi)

=

FV(\varphi)\setminus\{X\}
.

Proof.

Apply Recursion principle on patterns (Proposition
2.12
) with

D=2^{EVar\cup SVar}
and

\displaystyle G_{0}(\varphi)=EVar(\varphi)\cup SVar(\varphi),

\displaystyle\quad G_{\circ}(V_{1},V_{2},\varphi,\psi)=V_{1}\cup V_{2},

\displaystyle G_{\exists}(V,x,\varphi)=V\setminus\{x\},

\displaystyle\quad G_{\mu}(V,X,\varphi)=V\setminus\{X\}.

Then

(i)

FV(\varphi)=EVar(\varphi)\cup SVar(\varphi)=G_{0}(\varphi)
if
\varphi
is an atomic pattern.

(ii)

FV(\circ\varphi\psi)=FV(\varphi)\cup FV(\psi)=G_{\circ}(FV(\varphi),FV(\psi),\varphi,\psi)
for
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
.

(iii)

FV(\exists x\varphi)=FV(\varphi)\setminus\{x\}=G_{\exists}(FV(\varphi),x,\varphi)
.

(iv)

FV(\mu X\varphi)=FV(\varphi)\setminus\{X\}=G_{\mu}(FV(\varphi),X,\varphi)
.

Thus,
FV:Pattern\to 2^{EVar\cup SVar}
is the unique mapping given by Proposition
2.12
.
∎

2.5
Positive and negative occurences of set variables

Definition 2.20
.

Let
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
be a pattern and
X
be a set variable.

(i)

We say that
\to
is
an implication at the
i
th place of
\varphi
with left scope
\psi
and right scope
\chi
if

\varphi_{i}=\to
and
\psi=\varphi_{i+1}\ldots\varphi_{j}
,
\chi=\varphi_{j+1}\ldots\varphi_{l}
are the unique patterns given
by Proposition
2.11
.

(ii)

X

occurs left at the
k
th place of
\varphi
if
X
occurs free at the
k
th place of
\varphi

and there exist
0\leq i<k\leq j\leq n-1
such that
\psi=\varphi_{i+1}\ldots\varphi_{j}
is the left scope of an implication
\to
at the
i
th place of
\varphi
.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
X
be a set variable. We define the mapping

N_{X,L}:Pattern\to Fun({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{N}})

by recursion on patterns as follows:

(i)

\varphi
is an atomic pattern. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=0
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
.

(ii)

\varphi=Appl\psi\chi
, that is
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
with
\varphi_{0}=Appl
,
\psi=\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{j}
and
\chi=\varphi_{j+1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
for some

1\leq j<n-1
. We have the following cases:

(a)

k=0
or
k\geq n
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=0
.

(b)

1\leq k\leq j
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=N_{X,L}(\psi)(k-1)
.

(c)

j+1\leq k\leq n-1
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=N_{X,L}(\chi)(k-j-1)
.

(iii)

\varphi=\to\psi\chi
, that is
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
with
\varphi_{0}=\to
,
\psi=\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{j}
and
\chi=\varphi_{j+1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
for some

1\leq j<n-1
. We have the following cases:

(a)

k=0
or
k\geq n
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=0
.

(b)

1\leq k\leq j
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=N_{X,L}(\psi)(k-1)+1
.

(c)

j+1\leq k\leq n-1
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=N_{X,L}(\chi)(k-j-1)
.

(iv)

\varphi=\exists x\psi
, that is
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
with
\varphi_{0}=\exists
,
\varphi_{1}=x
and
\psi=\varphi_{2}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
.
We have the following cases:

(a)

k\in\{0,1\}
or
k\geq n
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=0
.

(b)

2\leq k\leq n-1
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=N_{X,L}(\psi)(k-2)
.

(v)

\varphi=\mu Z\psi
, that is
\varphi=\varphi_{0}\varphi_{1}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
with
\varphi_{0}=\mu
,
\varphi_{1}=Z
and
\psi=\varphi_{2}\ldots\varphi_{n-1}
. If

Z=X
, then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=0
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
. If
Z\neq X
, we have the following cases:

(a)

k\in\{0,1\}
or
k\geq n
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=0
.

(b)

2\leq k\leq n-1
. Then
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=N_{X,L}(\psi)(k-2)
.

Proof.

Apply
Recursion principle on patterns (Proposition
2.12
) with
D=Fun({\mathbb{N}},{\mathbb{N}})
and

\displaystyle G_{0}(\varphi)(k)

\displaystyle=0\qquad\text{~if~}\varphi\text{~is an atomic pattern},

\displaystyle G_{Appl}(f,g,\psi,\chi)(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{~if~}k=0\text{~or~}k\geq n,\\
f(k-1)&\text{~if~}1\leq k\leq j,\\
g(k-j-1)&\text{~if~}j+1\leq k\leq n-1,\\
\end{cases},

\displaystyle G_{\to}(f,g,\psi,\chi)(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{~if~}k=0\text{~or~}k\geq n,\\
f(k-1)+1&\text{~if~}1\leq k\leq j,\\
g(k-j-1)&\text{~if~}j+1\leq k\leq n-1,\\
\end{cases},

\displaystyle G_{\exists}(f,x,\psi)(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{~if~}k\in\{0,1\}\text{~or~}k\geq n,\\
f(k-2)&\text{~if~}2\leq k\leq n-1,\\
\end{cases},

\displaystyle G_{\mu}(f,Z,\psi)(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{~if~}X=Z,\\
0&\text{~if~}X\neq Z\text{~and~}(k\in\{0,1\}\text{~or~}k\geq n),\\
f(k-2)&\text{~if~}X\neq Z\text{~and~}2\leq k\leq n-1.\end{cases}

Then

(i)

N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)=0=G_{0}(\varphi)(k)
for every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
if
\varphi
is an atomic pattern.

(ii)

\varphi=Appl\psi\chi
. Then

\displaystyle N_{X,L}(\to\psi\chi)(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{~if~}k=0\text{~or~}k\geq n,\\
N_{X,L}(\psi)(k-1)&\text{~if~}1\leq k\leq j,\\
N_{X,L}(\chi)(k-j-1)&\text{~if~}j+1\leq k\leq n-1\end{cases}

\displaystyle=G_{Appl}(N_{X,L}(\psi),N_{X,L}(\chi),\psi,\chi)(k).

(iii)

\varphi=\to\psi\chi
. Then

\displaystyle N_{X,L}(\to\psi\chi)(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{~if~}k=0\text{~or~}k\geq n,\\
N_{X,L}(\psi)(k-1)+1&\text{~if~}1\leq k\leq j,\\
N_{X,L}(\chi)(k-j-1)&\text{~if~}j+1\leq k\leq n-1\end{cases}

\displaystyle=G_{Appl}(N_{X,L}(\psi),N_{X,L}(\chi),\psi,\chi)(k).

(iv)

\varphi=\exists x\psi
. Then

\displaystyle N_{X,L}(\exists x\psi)(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{~if~}k\in\{0,1\}\text{~or~}k\geq n,\\
N_{X,L}(\psi)(k-2)&\text{~if~}2\leq k\leq n-1\end{cases}

\displaystyle=G_{\exists}(N_{X,L}(\psi),x,\psi)(k).

(v)

\varphi=\mu Z\psi
. If
X=Z
, then
N_{X,L}(\mu Z\psi)(k)=0=G_{\mu}(N_{X,L}(\psi),Z,\psi)(k)
.

Assume that
X\neq Z
. Then

\displaystyle N_{X,L}(\mu Z\psi)(k)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{~if~}k\in\{0,1\}\text{~or~}k\geq n,\\
N_{X,L}(\psi)(k-2)&\text{~if~}2\leq k\leq n-1\end{cases}

\displaystyle=G_{\mu}(N_{X,L}(\psi),Z,\psi)(k).

∎

Notation 2.22
.

Let
X
be a set variable,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\varphi
be a pattern. We denote
N_{X,L}(\varphi)(k)

N_{L}(\varphi,X,k)
.

Definition 2.23
.

Let
X
be a set variable,
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\varphi
be a pattern such that
X
occurs free at the
k
th place of
\varphi
.

(i)

We say that
X

occurs positively at the
k
th place of
\varphi
(or that
X

has a positive occurence at the
k
th place of
\varphi
) if
N_{L}(\varphi,X,k)=0
or
N_{L}(\varphi,X,k)
is an even natural number.

(ii)

We say that
X

occurs negatively at the
k
th place of
\varphi
(or that
X

has a negative occurence at the
k
th place of
\varphi
) if
N_{L}(\varphi,X,k)
is an odd natural number.

Definition 2.24
.

We say that
\varphi
is
positive in
X
if one of the following is true:

(i)

X
does not occur free in
\varphi
.

(ii)

For every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
X
occurs free at the
k
th place of
\varphi
, then
X
occurs positively at the
k
th place of
\varphi
.

Definition 2.25
.

We say that
\varphi
is
negative in
X
if one of the following is true:

(i)

X
does not occur free in
\varphi
.

(ii)

For every
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, if
X
occurs free at the
k
th place of
\varphi
, then
X
occurs negatively at the
k
th place of
\varphi
.

Example 2.26
.

(i)

\varphi=\to X\to X\bot
. Then
N_{L}(X,\varphi,1)=N_{L}(X,X,0)+1=0+1=1
. Furthermore,

N_{L}(X,\varphi,3)=N_{L}(X,\to X\bot,1)=N_{L}(X,X,0)+1=1
. Thus,
\varphi
is negative in
X
.

(ii)

\varphi=\to\to X\bot\bot
. Then
N_{L}(X,\varphi,2)=N_{L}(X,\to X\bot,1)+1=N_{L}(X,X,0)+2=2
. Thus,

\varphi
is positive in
X
.

(iii)

\varphi=Appl{\to X\to X\bot}{\to\to X\bot\bot}
. Then
\varphi
is neither positive nor negative in
X
.

Remark 2.27

(Alternative definition)
.

The property that
\varphi
is positive in
X
can be defined by recursion on patterns as follows:

(i)

If
\varphi
is atomic, then
\varphi
is positive in
X
.

(ii)

If
\varphi=Appl\psi\chi
, then
\varphi
is positive in
X
iff both
\psi
,
\chi
are positive in
X
.

(iii)

If
\varphi=\to\psi\chi
, then
\varphi
is positive in
X
iff
\psi
is negative in
X
and
\chi
is positive in
X
.

(iv)

If
\varphi=\exists x\psi
, then
\varphi
is positive in
X
iff
\psi
is positive in
X
.

(v)

If
\varphi=\mu X\psi
, then
\varphi
is positive in
X
.

(vi)

If
\varphi=\mu Z\psi
with
Z\neq X
, then
\varphi
is positive in
X
iff
\psi
is positive in
X
.

Remark 2.28

(Alternative definition)
.

The property that
\varphi
is negative in
X
can be defined by recursion on patterns as follows:

(i)

If
\varphi
is atomic, then
\varphi
is negative in
X
iff
\varphi\neq X
.

(ii)

If
\varphi=Appl\psi\chi
, then
\varphi
is negative in
X
iff both
\psi
,
\chi
are negative in
X
.

(iii)

If
\varphi=\to\psi\chi
, then
\varphi
is negative in
X
iff
\psi
is positive in
X
and
\chi
is negative in
X
.

(iv)

If
\varphi=\exists x\psi
, then
\varphi
is negative in
X
iff
\psi
is negative in
X
.

(v)

If
\varphi=\mu X\psi
, then
\varphi
is negative in
X
.

(vi)

If
\varphi=\mu Z\psi
with
Z\neq X
, then
\varphi
is negative in
X
iff
\psi
is negative in
X
.

2.6
Substitution of element variables

Let
x
be an element variable and
\varphi
,
\delta
be patterns.

Definition 2.29
.

We define
Subf^{x}_{\delta}{\varphi}
to be the expression
obtained from
\varphi
by replacing every free occurence of
x
in
\varphi
with
\delta
.

Remark 2.30

(Alternative definition)
.

The mapping

Subf^{x}_{\delta}:Pattern\to Expr,\quad Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\varphi)=Subf^{x}_{\delta}{\varphi}

can be
defined by recursion on patterns as follows:

Subf^{x}_{\delta}(z)

=

\begin{cases}\delta\text{ if }x=z\\
z\text{ if }x\neq z\\
\end{cases}

if
z\in EVar
,

Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\varphi)

=

\varphi

if
\varphi\in SVar\cup\Sigma
,

Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\circ\psi\chi)

=

\circ Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\psi)Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\chi)

for
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
,

Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\exists z\psi)

=

\begin{cases}\exists z\psi&\text{ if }x=z\\
\exists zSubf^{x}_{\delta}(\psi)&\text{ if }x\neq z\\
\end{cases}
,

Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\mu X\psi)

=

\mu XSubf^{x}_{\delta}(\psi)
.

Proof.

Apply
Recursion principle on patterns (Proposition
2.12
) with
D=Expr
and

\displaystyle G_{0}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\delta&\text{~if~}\varphi=x\\
\varphi&\text{~if~}\varphi\in(EVar\setminus\{x\})\cup SVar\cup\Sigma\end{cases}

\displaystyle G_{\circ}(\theta,\tau,\psi,\chi)

\displaystyle=\circ\theta\tau\text{~for~}\circ\in\{Appl,\to\},

\displaystyle G_{\exists}(\theta,z,\psi)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\exists z\psi&\text{~if~}x=z\\
\exists z\theta&\text{~if~}x\neq z\\
\end{cases},

\displaystyle G_{\mu}(\theta,X,\psi)

\displaystyle=\mu X\theta.

Then

(i)

If
\varphi
is an atomic pattern, we have the following cases:

(a)

\varphi=x
. Then
Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\varphi)=Subf^{x}_{\delta}(x)=\delta=G_{0}(\varphi)
.

(b)

\varphi\in(EVar\setminus\{x\})\cup SVar\cup\Sigma
. Then
Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\varphi)=\varphi=G_{0}(\varphi)
.

(ii)

For
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
, we have that

Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\circ\psi\chi)=\circ Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\psi)Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\chi)=G_{\circ}(Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\psi),Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\chi),\psi,\chi).

(iii)

Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\exists z\psi)=\begin{cases}\exists z\psi&\text{ if }x=z\\
\exists zSubf^{x}_{\delta}(\psi)&\text{ if }x\neq z\\
\end{cases}=G_{\exists}(Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\psi),z,\psi)

(iv)

Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\mu X\psi)=\mu XSubf^{x}_{\delta}(\psi)=G_{\mu}(Subf^{x}_{\delta}(\psi),X,\psi)
.

Thus,
Subf^{x}_{\delta}
is the unique mapping given by Proposition
2.12
.
∎

Proposition 2.31
.

Subf^{x}_{\delta}{\varphi}
is a pattern.

Proof.

The proof is immediate by induction on
\varphi
, using the alternative definition
of
Subf^{x}_{\delta}{\varphi}
.∎

Example 2.32
.

[TRIVIAL CASES]

(i)

Subf^{x}_{x}{\varphi}=\varphi
.

(ii)

If
x
does not occur free in
\varphi
, then
Subf^{x}_{\delta}{\varphi}=\varphi
.

Definition 2.33
.

We say that
x
is
free for
\delta
in
\varphi
or that
\delta

is
substitutable for
x
in
\varphi
if the following hold:

(i)

if
z
is an element variable occuring free in
\delta
and
\exists
is a quantifier on
z
in
\varphi
with scope
\theta
, then

x
does not occur free in
\theta
.

(ii)

if
Z
is a set variable occuring free in
\delta
and
\mu
is a binder on
Z
in
\varphi

with scope
\theta
, then
x
does not occur free in
\theta
.

Example 2.34
.

x
is free for
\delta
in
\varphi
in any of the following cases:

(i)

x
does not occur free in
\varphi
.

(ii)

Any element or set variable of
\delta
does not occur bound in
\varphi
.

(iii)

EVar(\delta)=SVar(\delta)=\emptyset
.

Remark 2.35
.

Let
y\neq x
be an element variable.

(i)

x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
if the following holds: if
\exists
is a quantifier on
y
in
\varphi
with scope
\theta
, then

x
does not occur free in
\theta
.

(ii)

x
is not free for
y
in
\varphi
if
\exists
is a quantifier on
y
in
\varphi
with scope
\theta
and
x
occurs free in
\theta
.

2.6.1
Bounded substitution

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
x
,
y
be element variables.

Definition 2.36
.

We define
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi
to be the expression
obtained from
\varphi
by replacing every bound occurence of
x
in
\varphi
with
y
.

Remark 2.37

(Alternative definition)
.

If
x=y
, then obviously
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi=\varphi
. Assume that
x\neq y
. Then the mapping

Subb^{x}_{y}:Pattern\to Expr,\quad Subb^{x}_{y}(\varphi)=Subb^{x}_{y}{\varphi}

can be defined by recursion on patterns as follows:

Subb^{x}_{y}(\varphi)

=

\varphi

if
\varphi
is an atomic pattern,

Subb^{x}_{y}(\circ\psi\chi)

=

\circ Subb^{x}_{y}(\psi)Subb^{x}_{y}(\chi)

for
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
,

Subb^{x}_{y}(\exists z\psi)

=

\begin{cases}\exists ySubf^{x}_{y}{\left(Subb^{x}_{y}(\psi)\right)}&\text{ if }x=z\\
\exists zSubb^{x}_{y}(\psi)&\text{ if }x\neq z\\
\end{cases}
,

Subb^{x}_{y}(\mu X\psi)

=

\mu XSubb^{x}_{y}(\psi)
.

Proof.

Apply
Recursion principle on patterns (Proposition
2.12
) with
D=Expr
and

\displaystyle G_{0}(\varphi)=\varphi,

\displaystyle\quad G_{\circ}(\theta,\tau,\psi,\chi)=\circ\theta\tau\text{~for~}\circ\in\{Appl,\to\},

\displaystyle G_{\exists}(\theta,z,\psi)=\begin{cases}\exists ySubf^{x}_{y}{(\theta)}&\text{ if }x=z\\
\exists z\theta&\text{ if }x\neq z\\
\end{cases},

\displaystyle\quad G_{\mu}(\theta,X,\psi)=\mu X\theta.

Then

(i)

If
\varphi
is an atomic pattern,
Subb^{x}_{y}(\varphi)=\varphi=G_{0}(\varphi)
.

(ii)

For
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
, we have that

Subb^{x}_{y}(\circ\psi\chi)=\circ Subb^{x}_{y}(\psi)Subb^{x}_{y}(\chi)=G_{\circ}(Subb^{x}_{y}(\psi),Subb^{x}_{y}(\chi),\psi,\chi).

(iii)

Subb^{x}_{y}(\exists z\psi)=\begin{cases}\exists ySubf^{x}_{y}{\left(Subb^{x}_{y}(\psi)\right)}&\text{ if }x=z\\
\exists zSubb^{x}_{y}(\psi)&\text{ if }x\neq z\\
\end{cases}=G_{\exists}(Subb^{x}_{y}(\psi),z,\psi)

(iv)

Subb^{x}_{y}(\mu X\psi)=\mu XSubb^{x}_{y}(\psi)=G_{\mu}(Subb^{x}_{y}(\psi),X,\psi)
.

Thus,
Subb^{x}_{y}
is the unique mapping given by Proposition
2.12
.
∎

Proposition 2.38
.

Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi
is a pattern.

Proof.

The proof is immediate by induction on
\varphi
, using the alternative definition of
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi
.∎

Proposition 2.39
.

Assume that
x\neq y
and
y
does not occur in
\varphi
. Then
x
is free for
y
in
Subb^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\varphi
.

(i)

\varphi
is an atomic pattern. Then
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi=\varphi
and
x
is free for
y
in
\varphi
, as
y
does not occur in
\varphi
.

(ii)

\varphi=\circ\psi\chi
for
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
. Then
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi=\circ Subb^{x}_{y}(\psi)Subb^{x}_{y}(\chi)
.
As
y
does not occur in
\psi
,
\chi
,
we can apply the induction hypothesis to get that
x
is free for
y
in
Subb^{x}_{y}{\psi}
,
Subb^{x}_{y}{\chi}
.
It follows that
x
is free for
y
in
Subb^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
.

(iii)

\varphi=\exists z\psi
. We have two cases:

(a)

x=z
. Then
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi=\exists ySubf^{x}_{y}{\left(Subb^{x}_{y}(\psi)\right)}
. It is obvious that
x

does not occur in

Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi
, so
x
is free for
y
in
Subb^{x}_{y}{\varphi}
.

(b)

x\neq z
. Then
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi=\exists zSubb^{x}_{y}(\psi)
. As
y
does not occur in
\psi
, we
can apply the induction hypothesis to get that
x
is free for
y
in
Subb^{x}_{y}{\psi}
. As
y
does not occur in
\varphi
, we must
have that
y\neq z
. Then, obviously
x
is free for
y
in
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi
.

(iv)

\varphi=\mu X\psi
. Then
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi=\mu XSubb^{x}_{y}(\psi)
. As
y
does not occur in
\psi
, we
can apply the induction hypothesis to get that
x
is free for
y
in
Subb^{x}_{y}{\psi}
.
Then, obviously
x
is free for
y
in
Subb^{x}_{y}\varphi
.

∎

2.7
Substitution of set variables

Let
X
be a set variable and
\varphi
,
\delta
be patterns.

Definition 2.40
.

We define
Subf^{X}_{\delta}\varphi
to be the expression
obtained from
\varphi
by replacing every free occurence of
X
in
\varphi
with
\delta
.

Remark 2.41

(Alternative definition)
.

The mapping

Subf^{X}_{\delta}:Pattern\to Expr,\quad Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\varphi)=Subf^{X}_{\delta}{\varphi}

can be
defined by recursion on patterns as follows:

Subf^{X}_{\delta}(Z)

=

\begin{cases}\delta\text{ if }X=Z\\
Z\text{ if }X\neq Z\\
\end{cases}

if
Z\in SVar
,

Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\varphi)

=

\varphi

if
\varphi\in EVar\cup\Sigma
,

Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\circ\psi\chi)

=

\circ Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\psi)Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\chi)

for
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
,

Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\exists x\psi)

=

\exists xSubf^{X}_{\delta}(\psi)
,

Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\mu Z\psi)

=

\begin{cases}\mu Z\psi&\text{ if }X=Z\\
\mu ZSubf^{X}_{\delta}(\psi)&\text{ if }X\neq Z.\end{cases}

Proof.

Apply
Recursion principle on patterns (Proposition
2.12
) with
D=Expr
and

\displaystyle G_{0}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\delta&\text{~if~}\varphi=X\\
\varphi&\text{~if~}\varphi\in EVar\cup(SVar\setminus\{X\})\cup\Sigma\end{cases}

\displaystyle G_{\circ}(\theta,\tau,\psi,\chi)

\displaystyle=\circ\theta\tau\text{~for~}\circ\in\{Appl,\to\},

\displaystyle G_{\exists}(\theta,x,\psi)

\displaystyle=\exists x\theta,

\displaystyle G_{\mu}(\theta,Z,\psi)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mu Z\psi&\text{ if }X=Z\\
\mu Z\theta&\text{ if }X\neq Z.\end{cases}

Then

(i)

If
\varphi
is an atomic pattern, we have the following cases:

(a)

\varphi=X
. Then
Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\varphi)=Subf^{X}_{\delta}(X)=\delta=G_{0}(\varphi)
.

(b)

\varphi\in EVar\cup(SVar\setminus\{X\})\cup\Sigma
. Then
Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\varphi)=\varphi=G_{0}(\varphi)
.

(ii)

For
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
, we have that

Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\circ\psi\chi)=\circ Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\psi)Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\chi)=G_{\circ}(Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\psi),Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\chi),\psi,\chi).

(iii)

Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\exists x\psi)=\exists xSubf^{X}_{\delta}(\psi)=G_{\exists}(Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\psi),x,\psi).

(iv)

Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\mu Z\psi)=\begin{cases}\mu Z\psi&\text{ if }X=Z\\
\mu ZSubf^{X}_{\delta}(\psi)&\text{ if }X\neq Z.\end{cases}=G_{\mu}(Subf^{X}_{\delta}(\psi),Z,\psi)

Thus,
Subf^{X}_{\delta}
is the unique mapping given by Proposition
2.12
.
∎

Proposition 2.42
.

Subf^{X}_{\delta}{\varphi}
is a pattern.

Proof.

The proof is immediate by induction on
\varphi
, using the alternative definition
of
Subf^{X}_{\delta}{\varphi}
.∎

Definition 2.43
.

We say that
X
is
free for
\delta
in
\varphi
or that
\delta

is
substitutable for
X
in
\varphi
if the following hold:

(i)

if
z
is an element variable occuring free in
\delta
and
\exists
is a quantifier on
z
in
\varphi
with scope
\theta
, then

X
does not occur free in
\theta
.

(ii)

if
Z
is a set variable occuring free in
\delta
and
\mu
is a binder on
Z
in
\varphi
with scope
\theta
, then

X
does not occur free in
\theta
.

Example 2.44
.

X
is free for
\delta
in
\varphi
in any of the following cases:

(i)

X
does not occur free in
\varphi
.

(ii)

Any element or set variable of
\delta
does not occur bound in
\varphi
.

(iii)

EVar(\delta)=SVar(\delta)=\emptyset
.

2.7.1
Bounded substitution

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
X
,
Y
be set variables.

Definition 2.45
.

We define
Subb^{X}_{Y}\varphi
to be the expression
obtained from
\varphi
by replacing every bound occurence of
X
in
\varphi
with
Y
.

Remark 2.46

(Alternative definition)
.

If
X=Y
, then obviously
Subb^{X}_{Y}\varphi=\varphi
. Assume that
X\neq Y
. Then the mapping

Subb^{X}_{Y}:Pattern\to Expr,\quad Subb^{X}_{Y}(\varphi)=Subb^{X}_{Y}\varphi

can be defined by recursion on patterns as follows:

Subb^{X}_{Y}(\varphi)

=

\varphi

if
\varphi
is an atomic pattern,

Subb^{X}_{Y}(\circ\psi\chi)

=

\circ Subb^{X}_{Y}(\psi)Subb^{X}_{Y}(\chi)

for
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
,

Subb^{X}_{Y}(\exists x\psi)

=

\exists xSubb^{X}_{Y}(\psi)
,

Subb^{X}_{Y}(\mu Z\psi)

=

\begin{cases}\mu Y{Subf^{X}_{Y}{\left(Subb^{X}_{Y}(\psi)\right)}}&\text{ if }X=Z\\
\mu ZSubb^{X}_{Y}(\psi)&\text{ if }X\neq Z\\
\end{cases}
.

Proof.

Apply
Recursion principle on patterns (Proposition
2.12
) with
D=Expr
and

\displaystyle G_{0}(\varphi)=\varphi,

\displaystyle\quad G_{\circ}(\theta,\tau,\psi,\chi)=\circ\theta\tau\text{~for~}\circ\in\{Appl,\to\},

\displaystyle G_{\exists}(\theta,x,\psi)=\exists x\theta,

\displaystyle\quad G_{\mu}(\theta,Z,\psi)=\begin{cases}\mu Y{Subf^{X}_{Y}{(\theta)}}&\text{ if }X=Z\\
\mu Z\theta&\text{ if }X\neq Z.\end{cases}

Then

(i)

If
\varphi
is an atomic pattern, then
Subb^{X}_{Y}(\varphi)=\varphi=G_{0}(\varphi)
.

(ii)

For
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
, we have that

Subb^{X}_{Y}(\circ\psi\chi)=\circ Subb^{X}_{Y}(\psi)Subb^{X}_{Y}(\chi)=G_{\circ}(Subb^{X}_{Y}(\psi),Subb^{X}_{Y}(\chi),\psi,\chi).

(iii)

Subb^{X}_{Y}(\exists x\psi)=\exists xSubb^{X}_{Y}(\psi)=G_{\exists}(Subb^{X}_{Y}(\psi),x,\psi)
.

(iv)

Subb^{X}_{Y}(\mu Z\psi)=\begin{cases}\mu Y{Subf^{X}_{Y}{\left(Subb^{X}_{Y}(\psi)\right)}}&\text{ if }X=Z\\
\mu ZSubb^{X}_{Y}(\psi)&\text{ if }X\neq Z\end{cases}=G_{\mu}(Subb^{X}_{Y}(\psi),Z,\psi)
.

Thus,
Subb^{X}_{Y}
is the unique mapping given by Proposition
2.12
.
∎

Proposition 2.47
.

Subb^{X}_{Y}\varphi
is a pattern.

Proof.

The proof is immediate by induction on
\varphi
, using the alternative definition of
Subb^{X}_{Y}\varphi
.∎

Proposition 2.48
.

Assume that
X\neq Y
and
Y
does not occur in
\varphi
. Then
X
is free for
Y
in
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\varphi
.

(i)

\varphi
is an atomic pattern. Then
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}=\varphi
and
X
is free for
Y
in
\varphi
, as
Y
does not occur in
\varphi
.

(ii)

\varphi=\circ\psi\chi
for
\circ\in\{Appl,\to\}
. Then

Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}=ApplSubb^{X}_{Y}{\psi}Subb^{X}_{Y}{\chi}
. As
Y
does not occur in
\psi
,
\chi
,
we can apply the induction hypothesis to get that
X
is free for
Y
in
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\psi}
,
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\psi}
.
It follows that
X
is free for
Y
in
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}
.

(iii)

\varphi=\exists x\psi
. Then
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}=\exists xSubb^{X}_{Y}{\psi}
. As
Y
does not occur in
\psi
, we
can apply the induction hypothesis to get that
X
is free for
Y
in
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\psi}
.
Then, obviously
X
is free for
Y
in
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}
.

(iv)

\varphi=\mu Z\psi
.
We have two cases:

(a)

X=Z
. Then
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}=\mu Y{Subf^{X}_{Y}{\left(Subb^{X}_{Y}{\psi}\right)}}
. It is obvious that
X
does not occur in

Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}
, so
X
is free for
Y
in
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}
.

(b)

X\neq Z
. Then
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}=\mu ZSubb^{X}_{Y}{\psi}
. As
Y
does not occur in
\psi
, we
can apply the induction hypothesis to get that
X
is free for
Y
in
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\psi}
. As
Y
does not occur in
\varphi
, we must
have that
Y\neq Z
. Then, obviously
X
is free for
Y
in
Subb^{X}_{Y}{\varphi}
.

∎

2.8
Free substitution

Definition 2.49
.

Let
x
be an element variable and
\varphi
,
\delta
be patterns.
We define
\varphi_{x}(\delta)
as follows:

(i)

If
x
is free for
\delta
in
\varphi
, then
\varphi_{x}(\delta)=Subf^{x}_{\delta}\varphi
.

(ii)

Assume that
x
is not free for
\delta
in
\varphi
and let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
be the element
variables and
U_{1},\ldots,U_{p}
be the set variables that occur bound in
\varphi
and also
occur in
\delta
. Let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
be new element variables and
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{p}
be
new set variables, that do not occur in
\varphi
or
\delta
. Consider the pattern

\theta=Subb^{U_{1}}_{Z_{1}}Subb^{U_{2}}_{Z_{2}}\ldots Subb^{U_{p}}_{Z_{p}}Subb^{u_{1}}_{z_{1}}Subb^{u_{2}}_{z_{2}}\ldots Subb^{u_{k}}_{z_{k}}\varphi.

Then
\varphi_{x}(\delta)=Subf^{x}_{\delta}{\theta}
.

Definition 2.50
.

Let
X
be a set variable and
\varphi
,
\delta
be patterns.
We define
\varphi_{X}(\delta)
as follows:

(i)

If
X
is free for
\delta
in
\varphi
, then
\varphi_{X}(\delta)=Subf^{X}_{\delta}\varphi
.

(ii)

Assume that
X
is not free for
\delta
in
\varphi
and let
u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
be the element
variables and
U_{1},\ldots,U_{p}
be the set variables that occur bound in
\varphi
and also
occur in
\delta
. Let
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
be new element variables and
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{p}
be
new set variables, that do not occur in
\varphi
or
\delta
. Consider the pattern

\theta=Subb^{U_{1}}_{Z_{1}}Subb^{U_{2}}_{Z_{2}}\ldots Subb^{U_{p}}_{Z_{p}}Subb^{u_{1}}_{z_{1}}Subb^{u_{2}}_{z_{2}}\ldots Subb^{u_{k}}_{z_{k}}\varphi.

Then
\varphi_{X}(\delta)=Subf^{X}_{\delta}{\theta}
.

Remark 2.51
.

In the above definitions, we could define
z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
and
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{p}
as follows:

(i)

Let
l\in{\mathbb{N}}
be maximum such that
v_{l}
occurs in
\varphi
or
\delta
.
Then define
z_{i}=v_{l+i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
.

(ii)

Let
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
be maximum such that
V_{m}
occurs in
\varphi
or
\delta
.
Then define
Z_{i}=V_{m+i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,p
.

2.9
Contexts

Let
\Box
be a new symbol and let us denote

Sym_{\Box}=Sym_{\tau}\cup\{\Box\}\cup\{Appl_{\Box}\}.

Definition 2.52
.

The
\tau
-contexts
are the expressions over
Sym_{\Box}
inductively defined
as follows:

(i)

\Box
is a
\tau
-context.

(ii)

If
C
is a
\tau
-context and
\varphi
is a
\tau
-pattern, then

Appl_{\Box}C\varphi
and
Appl_{\Box}\varphi C
are
\tau
-contexts.

(iii)

Only the expressions over
Sym_{\Box}
obtained by applying the above rules are
\tau
-contexts.

The set of
\tau
-contexts is denoted by
\mathcal{C}_{\tau}
and
\tau
-contexts are denoted by
C
,
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, ….

Remark 2.53
.

The definition of
\tau
-contexts can be written using the BNF notation:

\displaystyle C::=

\displaystyle\Box\mid Appl_{\Box}C\varphi\mid Appl_{\Box}\varphi C.

Definition 2.54

(Alternative definition for
\tau
-contexts)
.

The set of
\tau
-contexts
is the intersection of all
sets
\Gamma
of expressions over
Sym_{\Box}
that have the following properties:

(i)

\Box\in\Gamma
.

(ii)

If
C\in\Gamma
and
\varphi
is a
\tau
-pattern, then
Appl_{\Box}C\varphi,Appl_{\Box}\varphi C\in\Gamma
.

Proposition 2.55
.

[Induction principle on contexts]

Let
\Gamma
be a set of
\tau
-contexts satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\Box\in\Gamma
.

(ii)

If
C\in\Gamma
and
\varphi
is a pattern, then
Appl_{\Box}C\varphi,Appl_{\Box}\varphi C\in\Gamma
.

Then
\Gamma=\mathcal{C}
.

Proof.

By hypothesis,
\Gamma\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. By Definition
2.54
, we get that

\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Gamma
.
∎

When the signature
\tau
is clear from the context, we shall say simply context(s) and
we shall denote the set of contexts by
\mathcal{C}
.

Proposition 2.56
.

\Box
occurs exactly once in every context
C
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the context
C
:

(i)

C=\Box
. Obviously.

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\psi
and, by the induction hypothesis,
\Box
occurs exactly once in
C_{1}
. As
\psi

is a pattern,
\Box
does not occur in
\psi
. Thus,
\Box
occurs exactly once in
C
.

(iii)

C=Appl_{\Box}\psi C_{1}
and, by the induction hypothesis,
\Box
occurs exactly once in
C_{1}
. As
\psi

is a pattern,
\Box
does not occur in
\psi
. Thus,
\Box
occurs exactly once in
C
.

∎

2.9.1
Unique readability and recursion principle

Proposition 2.57

(Unique readability of contexts)
.

(i)

Any context has a positive length.

(ii)

If
C
is a context, then one of the following hold:

(a)

C=\Box
.

(b)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\varphi
, where
C_{1}
is a context and
\varphi
is a pattern.

(c)

C=Appl_{\Box}\varphi C_{1}
, where
C_{1}
is a context and
\varphi
is a pattern.

(iii)

Any proper initial segment of a context is not a context.

(iv)

If
C
is a context, then exactly one of the cases from (
ii
)
holds. Moreover,
C
can be written in a unique way in one of these forms.

Proof.

Similarly with the proof of Proposition
2.9
.∎

Proposition 2.58

(Recursion principle on contexts)
.

Let
A
be a set,
\Box^{A}\in A
and the mappings

\displaystyle G_{1},G_{2}:A\times Pattern\to A.

Then there exists a unique mapping

F:\mathcal{C}\to A

that satisfies the following properties:

(i)

F(\Box)=\Box^{A}
for any atomic pattern
\varphi
.

(ii)

F(Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\varphi)=G_{1}(F(C_{1}),\varphi)
for any context
C_{1}
and any pattern
\varphi
.

(iii)

F(Appl_{\Box}\varphi C_{1})=G_{2}(F(C_{1}),\varphi)
for any context
C_{1}
and any pattern
\varphi
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
2.57
.
∎

2.9.2
Context substitution

Definition 2.59
.

Let
C
be a context and
\delta
be a pattern. We denote by
C[\delta]
the
\tau
-expression
obtained by replacing all the occurences of
\Box
with
\delta
and all occurences of

Appl_{\Box}
with
Appl
.

Remark 2.60

(Alternative definition)
.

C[\delta]
can be defined by recursion on contexts as follows:

\Box[\delta]

=

\delta
,

(Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\varphi)[\delta]

=

ApplC_{1}[\delta]\varphi
,

(Appl_{\Box}\varphi C_{1})[\delta]

=

Appl\varphi C_{1}[\delta]
.

Proof.

Let
F:\mathcal{C}\to Fun(Pattern,Expr_{\tau})
be defined by
F(C)(\delta)=C[\delta]
.
We show in the sequel that
F
can be defined by recursion on patterns. The above definitions
become

F(\Box)(\delta)=\delta,\quad F(Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\varphi)(\delta)=ApplF(C_{1})(\delta)\varphi,\quad F(Appl_{\Box}\varphi C_{1})(\delta)=Appl\varphi F(C_{1})(\delta).

Apply Proposition
2.58
with

A=Fun(Pattern,Expr_{\tau})
,

\Box^{A}(\delta)=\delta
,
G_{1}(f,\varphi)(\delta)=Applf(\delta)\varphi
and
G_{2}(f,\varphi)(\delta)=Appl\varphi f(\delta)

for every
f:Pattern\to Expr_{\tau}
and every patterns
\delta
,
\varphi
.
Then

(i)

F(\Box)(\delta)=\delta=\Box^{A}(\delta)
for every pattern
\delta
. It follows that
F(\Box)=\Box^{A}
.

(ii)

F(Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\varphi)(\delta)=ApplF(C_{1})(\delta)\varphi=G_{1}(F(C_{1}),\varphi)(\delta)
for every pattern
\delta
.
It follows that
F(Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\varphi)=G_{1}(F(C_{1}),\varphi)
.

(iii)

F(Appl_{\Box}\varphi C_{1})(\delta)=Appl\varphi F(C_{1})(\delta)=G_{2}(F(C_{1}),\varphi)(\delta)
for every pattern
\delta
.
It follows that
F(Appl_{\Box}\varphi C_{1})=G_{2}(F(C_{1}),\varphi)
.

Thus,
F
is the unique mapping given by Proposition
2.58
.
∎

Proposition 2.61
.

C[\delta]
is a pattern.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on the context
C
, using the alternative definition
of
C[\delta]
.

(i)

C=\Box
. Then
C[\delta]=\delta
is a pattern.

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\varphi
and, by the induction hypothesis,
C_{1}[\delta]
is a pattern. As

C[\delta]=ApplC_{1}[\delta]\varphi
, it follows that
C[\delta]
is a pattern.

(iii)

C=Appl_{\Box}\varphi C_{1}
and, by the induction hypothesis,
C_{1}[\delta]
is a pattern.
As
C[\delta]=Appl\varphi C_{1}[\delta]
, it follows that
C[\delta]
is a pattern.

∎

2.9.3
Free variables of contexts

Definition 2.62
.

The set
FV(C)
of
free variables
of
C
is defined by recursion on contexts as follows:

FV(\Box)

=

\emptyset
,

FV(Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\varphi)

=

FV(C_{1})\cup FV(\varphi)
,

FV(Appl_{\Box}\varphi C_{1})

=

FV(C_{1})\cup FV(\varphi)
.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
2.58
with

A=2^{EVar\cup SVar},\quad\Box^{A}=\emptyset,\quad G_{1}(V,\varphi)=G_{2}(V,\varphi)=V\cup FV(\varphi).

Then

(i)

FV(\Box)=\emptyset=\Box^{A}
.

(ii)

FV(Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\varphi)=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(\varphi)=G_{1}(FV(C_{1}),\varphi)
.

(iii)

FV(Appl_{\Box}\varphi C_{1})=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(\varphi)=G_{2}(FV(C_{1}),\varphi)
.

Thus,
FV:\mathcal{C}\to 2^{EVar\cup SVar}
is the unique mapping given by Proposition
2.58
.
∎

2.10
Derived connectives. Conventions

We shall write in the sequel
{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}
instead of
Appl\varphi\psi
,
\varphi\to\psi
instead of
\to\varphi\psi
,

\exists x.\varphi
instead of
\exists x\varphi
, and
\mu X.\varphi
instead of
\mu X\varphi
.
We shall also use parentheses when it is necessary to make clear the
structure of a pattern. For example, we write
{(\varphi\to\psi)}\cdot{\chi}
.

Furthermore, from now on,
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\ldots
will denote patterns

The pattern
{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}
is called an
application
. As a convention, application is
associative to the left. Thus, if
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}
are patterns, we write
{{{\varphi_{1}}\cdot{\varphi_{2}}}\cdot{\varphi_{3}}\ldots}\cdot{\varphi_{n}}
instead of

(\ldots{({({\varphi_{1}}\cdot{\varphi_{2}})}\cdot{\varphi_{3})}\ldots}\cdot{\varphi_{n}})
.

We introduce derived connectives by the following abbreviations:

\begin{array}[]{lll}\bot=\mu X.X&\neg\varphi=\varphi\to\bot,&\top=\neg\bot,\\[2.84526pt]
\varphi\vee\psi=\neg\varphi\to\psi,&\varphi\wedge\psi=\neg(\neg\varphi\vee\neg\psi),&\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi=(\varphi\to\psi)\wedge(\psi\to\varphi),\\[2.84526pt]
\forall x.\varphi=\neg\exists x.\neg\varphi,&\nu X.\varphi=\neg\mu X.\neg{Subf^{X}_{\neg X}{\varphi}}.\end{array}

The scope of
\exists
,
\forall
,
\mu
,
\nu
goes as far as possible to the right unless it
is limited by parentheses. For example,
\forall x.\varphi\rightarrow\psi
is
\forall x.(\varphi\rightarrow\psi)
,

\mu X.\varphi\wedge\psi
is
\mu X.(\varphi\wedge\psi)
, and

\exists x.\psi\rightarrow((\exists y.\varphi)\rightarrow z)
is
\exists x.\big(\psi\rightarrow((\exists y.\varphi)\rightarrow z)\big)
.

To reduce the use of parentheses, we assume that

(i)

application has the highest precedence.

(ii)

\mu
,
\nu
have higher precedence than the quantifiers
\exists
,
\forall
.

(iii)

quantifiers
\exists
,
\forall
have higher precedence than the propositional connectives.

(iv)

\neg
has higher precedence than
\rightarrow
,
\wedge
,
\vee
,
\leftrightarrow
;

(v)

\wedge,\vee
have higher precedence than
\rightarrow
,
\leftrightarrow
.

We define in the sequel
finite conjunctions and disjunctions
. Let
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}(n\geq 1)
be patterns.
Then
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}
and
\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}
are defined inductively as follows:

\displaystyle\bigwedge_{i=1}^{1}\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{1},\,\,\bigwedge_{i=1}^{2}\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2},\quad\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n+1}\varphi_{i}=\left(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}\right)\wedge\varphi_{n+1},

(3)

\displaystyle\bigvee_{i=1}^{1}\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{1},\,\,\bigvee_{i=1}^{2}\varphi_{i}=\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2},\quad\bigvee_{i=1}^{n+1}\varphi_{i}=\left(\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}\right)\vee\varphi_{n+1}.

(4)

We also write
\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{n}
instead of
\bigwedge_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}
and

\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}\vee\ldots\vee\varphi_{n}
instead of
\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\varphi_{i}
.

3
Semantics

Let
\tau=(EVar,SVar,\Sigma)
be a signature.

Definition 3.1
.

A
\tau
-structure is a pair

\mathcal{A}=(A,\_\star\_\,,\Sigma^{\cal A}),

where

(i)

A
is a nonempty set.

(ii)

\_\star\_:A\times A\to 2^{A}
is a binary
application
function;

(iii)

\Sigma^{\cal A}=\{\sigma^{\cal A}\subseteq A\mid\sigma\in\Sigma\}
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\tau
-structure.
A
is called the
universe
or the
domain
of the structure
\mathcal{A}
.
We use the notation
A=|\mathcal{A}|
. If
a\in A
, we also write
a\in\mathcal{A}
. Furthermore,
\sigma^{\mathcal{A}}
is called the

interpretation
or the
denotation
of the constant
\sigma
in
\mathcal{A}
.
We extend the application function
\_\star\_
as follows:

\_\star\_:2^{A}\times 2^{A}\to 2^{A},\quad B\star C=\bigcup_{b\in B,c\in C}b\star c.

Definition 3.2
.

An
\mathcal{A}
-valuation
is a function
e:EVar\cup SVar\to A\cup 2^{A}

that satisfies:

e(x)\in A
for all
x\in EVar
and
e(X)\subseteq A
for all
X\in SVar
.

\mathcal{A}
-valuations
are also called
\mathcal{A}
-assignments
.

In the following,
\mathcal{A}
is a
\tau
-structure and
e
is an
\mathcal{A}
-valuation.

Notation 3.3
.

For any variable
x\in EVar
and any
a\in A
, we define a new
\mathcal{A}
-valuation
e_{x\mapsto a}

as follows: for all
v\in EVar
and all
V\in SVar
,

e_{x\mapsto a}(V)=e(V)
and
e_{x\mapsto a}(v)=\begin{cases}e(v)&\text{ if }v\neq x\\
a&\text{ if }v=x.\end{cases}

For any variable
X\in SVar
and any
B\subseteq A
, we define a new
\mathcal{A}
-valuation

e_{X\mapsto{B}}
as follows: for all
v\in EVar
and all
V\in SVar
,

e_{X\mapsto{B}}(v)=e(v)
and
e_{X\mapsto{B}}(V)=\begin{cases}e(V)&\text{ if }V\neq X\\
B&\text{ if }V=X.\end{cases}

Definition 3.4
.

The
patterns
\mathcal{A}
-valuation

e^{+}:Pattern\to 2^{A}

is defined by recursion on patterns as follows:

(i)

e^{+}(x)=\{e(x)\}
if
x\in EVar
.

(ii)

e^{+}(X)=e(X)
if
X\in SVar
.

(iii)

e^{+}(\sigma)=\sigma^{\mathcal{A}}
for every
\sigma\in\Sigma
.

(iv)

e^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})=e^{+}(\varphi)\star e^{+}(\psi)
.

(v)

e^{+}(\varphi\to\psi)=C_{A}(e^{+}(\varphi)\setminus e^{+}(\psi))
.

(vi)

e^{+}(\exists x.\varphi)=\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)
.

(vii)

e^{+}(\mu X.\varphi)=\bigcap\left\{B\subseteq A\mid\left(e_{X\mapsto{B}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq B\right\}.

We say that
\varphi
evaluates to
B\subseteq A
(with
e
) if
e^{+}(\varphi)=B
. If
a\in e^{+}(\varphi)
,
we say that
a
matches
\varphi
(with witness
e
).

Proposition 3.5
.

For all patterns
\varphi,\psi
,

(i)

e^{+}(\varphi\to\psi)=C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\psi)
.

(ii)

e^{+}(\bot)=\emptyset
.

(iii)

e^{+}(\neg\varphi)=C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)
.

(iv)

e^{+}(\top)=A
.

(v)

e^{+}(\varphi\vee\psi)=e^{+}(\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\psi)
.

(vi)

e^{+}(\varphi\wedge\psi)=e^{+}(\varphi)\cap e^{+}(\psi)
.

(vii)

e^{+}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)=A\setminus(e^{+}(\varphi)\Delta e^{+}(\psi))
.

(viii)

e^{+}(\forall x.\varphi)=\bigcap_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)
.

Proof.

(i)

We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\varphi\to\psi)=C_{A}(e^{+}(\varphi)\setminus e^{+}(\psi))=C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\psi).

(ii)

We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\bot)

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\mu X.X)=\bigcap\left\{B\subseteq A\mid\left(e_{X\mapsto{B}}\right)^{+}(X)\subseteq B\right\}=\bigcap\left\{B\subseteq A\mid e_{X\mapsto{B}}(X)\subseteq B\right\}

\displaystyle=\bigcap\left\{B\subseteq A\mid B\subseteq B\right\}=\bigcap\left\{B\subseteq A\right\}=\emptyset.

(iii)

e^{+}(\neg\varphi)=e^{+}(\varphi\to\bot)=C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\bot)=C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)\cup\emptyset=C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)
.

(iv)

e^{+}(\top)=e^{+}(\neg\bot)=C_{A}e^{+}(\bot)=C_{A}\emptyset=A
.

(v)

We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\varphi\vee\psi)

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\neg\varphi\to\psi)=C_{A}e^{+}(\neg\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\psi)=C_{A}(C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi))\cup e^{+}(\psi)

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\psi).

(vi)

We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\varphi\wedge\psi)

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\neg(\neg\varphi\vee\neg\psi))=C_{A}e^{+}(\neg\varphi\vee\neg\psi)=C_{A}(e^{+}(\neg\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\neg\psi))

\displaystyle=C_{A}(C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)\cup C_{A}e^{+}(\psi))=e^{+}(\varphi)\cap e^{+}(\psi).

(vii)

We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)

\displaystyle=e^{+}\big((\varphi\to\psi)\wedge(\psi\to\varphi)\big)=e^{+}(\varphi\to\psi)\cap e^{+}(\psi\to\varphi)

\displaystyle=C_{A}(e^{+}(\varphi)\setminus e^{+}(\psi))\cap C_{A}(e^{+}(\psi)\setminus e^{+}(\varphi))=C_{A}(e^{+}(\varphi)\Delta e^{+}(\psi)).

(viii)

We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\forall x.\varphi)

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\neg\exists x.\neg\varphi)=C_{A}e^{+}(\exists x.\neg\varphi)=C_{A}\left(\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\neg\varphi)\right)

\displaystyle=C_{A}\left(\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(C_{A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\right)\right)=\bigcap_{a\in A}C_{A}\left(C_{A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\right)=\bigcap_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi).

∎

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern. We say that

(i)

e

satisfies

\varphi
in
\mathcal{A}

if
e^{+}(\varphi)=A
.

Notation:
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
.

(ii)

e

does not satisfy

\varphi
in
\mathcal{A}

if
e^{+}(\varphi)\neq A
.

Notation:
\mathcal{A}\not\vDash\varphi[e]
.

Proposition 3.7
.

(i)

For every element variable
x
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash x[e]
iff
|A|=1
.

(ii)

For every set variable
X
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash X[e]
iff
e(X)=A
.

(iii)

For every constant
\sigma
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\sigma[e]
iff
\sigma^{\mathcal{A}}=A
.

(iv)

\mathcal{A}\not\vDash\bot[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash\top[e]
.

(v)

For every pattern
\varphi
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\neg\varphi[e]
iff
e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset
.

(vi)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})[e]
iff

\bigcup_{b\in e^{+}(\varphi),c\in e^{+}(\psi)}b\star c=A
.

(vii)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\wedge\psi)[e]
iff (
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
).

(viii)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\vee\psi)[e]
iff
e^{+}(\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\psi)=A
.

(ix)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
iff
e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
.

(x)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)[e]
iff
e^{+}(\varphi)=e^{+}(\psi)
.

(xi)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)[e]
iff (
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]

and
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\psi\to\varphi)[e])
.

(xii)

For every pattern
\varphi
and every element variable
x
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\exists x.\varphi)[e]

iff
\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)=A
.

(xiii)

For every pattern
\varphi
and every element variable
x
, if there exists
b\in A
such that

\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{x\mapsto b}]
, then

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\exists x.\varphi)[e]
.

(xiv)

For every pattern
\varphi
and every element variable
x
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\forall x.\varphi)[e]
iff

\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{x\mapsto a}]
for every
a\in A
.

(xv)

For every pattern
\varphi
and every set variable
X
, if
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{X\mapsto{B}}]
for every
B\subseteq A
, then

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\mu X.\varphi)[e]
.

Proof.

(i)

\mathcal{A}\vDash x[e]
iff
e^{+}(x)=A
iff
\{e(x)\}=A
iff
|A|=1
.

(ii)

\mathcal{A}\vDash X[e]
iff
e^{+}(X)=A
iff
e(X)=A
.

(iii)

\mathcal{A}\vDash\sigma[e]
iff
e^{+}(\sigma)=A
iff
\sigma^{\mathcal{A}}=A
.

(iv)

Obviously, since
e^{+}(\bot)=\emptyset
and
e^{+}(\top)=A
.

(v)

\mathcal{A}\vDash\neg\varphi[e]
iff
e^{+}(\neg\varphi)=A
iff
A\setminus e^{+}(\varphi)=A
iff

e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset
.

(vi)

\mathcal{A}\vDash({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})[e]
iff
e^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})=A
iff
e^{+}(\varphi)\star e^{+}(\psi)=A

iff
\bigcup_{b\in e^{+}(\varphi),c\in e^{+}(\psi)}b\star c=A
.

(vii)

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\wedge\psi)[e]
iff
e^{+}(\varphi\wedge\psi)=A
iff
e^{+}(\varphi)\cap e^{+}(\psi)=A
iff

e^{+}(\varphi)=e^{+}(\psi)=A
(since
e^{+}(\varphi),e^{+}(\psi)\subseteq A
) iff (
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
).

(viii)

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\vee\psi)[e]
iff
e^{+}(\varphi\vee\psi)=A
iff
e^{+}(\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\psi)=A
.

(ix)

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
iff
e^{+}(\varphi\to\psi)=A
iff
C_{A}(e^{+}(\varphi)\setminus e^{+}(\psi))=A
iff

e^{+}(\varphi)\setminus e^{+}(\psi)=\emptyset
iff
e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
.

(x)

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)[e]
iff
e^{+}(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)=A
iff

A\setminus(e^{+}(\varphi)\Delta e^{+}(\psi))=A
iff
e^{+}(\varphi)\Delta e^{+}(\psi)=\emptyset

iff
e^{+}(\varphi)=e^{+}(\psi)
.

(xi)

Apply (
vii
).

(xii)

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\exists x.\varphi)[e]
iff
e^{+}(\exists x.\varphi)=A
iff
\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)=A
.

(xiii)

Assume that
b\in A
is such that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{x\mapsto b}]
, so
\left(e_{x\mapsto b}\right)^{+}(\varphi)=A
. Since
\left(e_{x\mapsto b}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)=e^{+}(\exists x.\varphi)
, we must have
e^{+}(\exists x.\varphi)=A
, hence

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\exists x.\varphi)[e]
.

(xiv)

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\forall x\varphi)[e]
iff
e^{+}(\forall x.\varphi)=A
iff
\bigcap_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)=A
iff
for every
a\in A
,
\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)=A
iff for every
a\in A
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{x\mapsto a}]
.

(xv)

Let us denote
\mathcal{B}=\left\{B\subseteq A\mid\left(e_{X\mapsto{B}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq B\right\}
. Obviously,
A\in\mathcal{B}
.
Assume that for every
B\subseteq A
we have that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{X\mapsto{B}}]
, hence
\left(e_{X\mapsto{B}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)=A
. It follows that
\mathcal{B}=\{A\}
,
since if
B\subseteq A,B\neq A
, then

\left(e_{X\mapsto{B}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)=A\not\subseteq B
. Thus,
e^{+}(\mu X.\varphi)=\bigcap\mathcal{B}=A
, so
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\mu X.\varphi)[e]
.

∎

Proposition 3.8
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\tau
-structure. Then for every pattern
\varphi
, the following holds:

(\star)
for every
\mathcal{A}
-valuations
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
, if
e_{1}|_{FV(\varphi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\varphi)}
, then

e_{1}^{+}(\varphi)=e_{2}^{+}(\varphi)
.

Proof.

We prove
(\star)
by induction on
\varphi
.

(i)

\varphi=x\in EVar
. Then
FV(\varphi)=\{x\}
. Let
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
be two
\mathcal{A}
-valuations such that

e_{1}(x)=e_{2}(x)
. We get that

e_{1}^{+}(\varphi)=\{e_{1}(x)\}=\{e_{2}(x)\}=e_{2}^{+}(\varphi)
.

(ii)

\varphi=X\in SVar
. Then
FV(\varphi)=\{X\}
. Let
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
be two
\mathcal{A}
-valuations such that

e_{1}(X)=e_{2}(X)
. We get that

e_{1}^{+}(\varphi)=e_{1}(X)=e_{2}(X)=e_{2}^{+}(\varphi)
.

(iii)

\varphi=\sigma\in\Sigma
. Then
FV(\varphi)=\emptyset
. Let
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
be two
\mathcal{A}
-valuations. Then

e_{1}^{+}(\varphi)=\sigma^{\mathcal{A}}=e_{2}^{+}(\varphi)
.

(iv)

\varphi={\psi}\cdot{\chi}
. Then
FV(\varphi)=FV(\psi)\cup FV(\chi)
. Let
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
be two
\mathcal{A}
-valuations such that

e_{1}|_{FV(\varphi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\varphi)}
. Then
e_{1}|_{FV(\psi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\psi)}
and

e_{1}|_{FV(\chi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\chi)}
. Applying the induction hypothesis for
\psi
,
\chi
,
it follows that
e_{1}^{+}(\psi)=e_{2}^{+}(\psi)
and
e_{1}^{+}(\chi)=e_{2}^{+}(\chi)
. We get that

e_{1}^{+}(\varphi)=e_{1}^{+}(\psi)\star e_{1}^{+}(\chi)=e_{2}^{+}(\psi)\star e_{2}^{+}(\chi)=e_{2}^{+}(\varphi
.)

(v)

\varphi=\psi\to\chi
. Then
FV(\varphi)=FV(\psi)\cup FV(\chi)
. Let
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
be two
\mathcal{A}
-valuations such that

e_{1}|_{FV(\varphi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\varphi)}
. Then
e_{1}|_{FV(\psi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\psi)}
and

e_{1}|_{FV(\chi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\chi)}
. Applying the induction hypothesis for
\psi
,
\chi
,
it follows that
e_{1}^{+}(\psi)=e_{2}^{+}(\psi)
and
e_{1}^{+}(\chi)=e_{2}^{+}(\chi)
. We get that

e_{1}^{+}(\varphi)=C_{A}(e_{1}^{+}(\psi)\setminus e_{1}^{+}(\chi))=C_{A}(e_{2}^{+}(\psi)\setminus e_{2}^{+}(\chi))=e_{2}^{+}(\varphi)
.

(vi)

\varphi=\exists x.\psi
. Then
FV(\varphi)=FV(\psi)\setminus\{x\}
. Let
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
be two
\mathcal{A}
-valuations such that

e_{1}|_{FV(\varphi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\varphi)}
. For every
a\in A
, we have that

{e_{1}}_{x\mapsto a}|_{FV(\psi)}={e_{2}}_{x\mapsto a}|_{FV(\psi)}
, hence, applying the induction hypothesis for
\psi
, that

\left({e_{1}}_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)=\left({e_{2}}_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)
. We get that

e_{1}^{+}(\varphi)=\bigcup_{a\in A}\left({e_{1}}_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)=\bigcup_{a\in A}\left({e_{2}}_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)=e_{2}^{+}(\varphi)
.

(vii)

\varphi=\mu X.\psi
. Then
FV(\varphi)=FV(\psi)\setminus\{X\}
. Let
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
be two
\mathcal{A}
-valuations such that

e_{1}|_{FV(\varphi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\varphi)}
. For every
B\subseteq A
, we have that

{e_{1}}_{X\mapsto B}|_{FV(\psi)}={e_{2}}_{X\mapsto B}|_{FV(\psi)}
, hence, applying the induction hypothesis for
\psi
, that

\left({e_{1}}_{X\mapsto B}\right)^{+}(\psi)=\left({e_{2}}_{X\mapsto B}\right)^{+}(\psi)
. We get that

e_{1}^{+}(\varphi)=\bigcap\{B\subseteq A\mid\left({e_{1}}_{X\mapsto B}\right)^{+}(\psi)\subseteq B\}=\bigcap\{B\subseteq A\mid\left({e_{2}}_{X\mapsto B}\right)^{+}(\psi)\subseteq B\}=e_{2}^{+}(\varphi)
.

∎

Hence, the
\mathcal{A}
-valuation of a pattern depends only on the
\mathcal{A}
-valuations of the free variables of the pattern. As an immediate
application of Proposition
3.8
, we get

Corollary 3.9
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\tau
-structure,
\varphi
be a pattern and
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
be two
\mathcal{A}
-valuations such that

e_{1}|_{FV(\varphi)}=e_{2}|_{FV(\varphi)}
.
Then
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{1}]
iff
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{2}]
.

3.1
Model, validity, satisfiability

Definition 3.10
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
\mathcal{A}
be a
\tau
-structure. We say that

\mathcal{A}

satisfies

\varphi
or that
\mathcal{A}
is a
model
of
\varphi
if
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]

for every
\mathcal{A}
-valuation
e
.

Notation:
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\tau
-structure.

(i)

For every element variable
x
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash x
iff
|A|=1
.

(ii)

For every constant symbol
\sigma
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\sigma
iff
\sigma^{\mathcal{A}}=A
.

(iii)

\mathcal{A}\not\vDash\bot
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash\top
.

(iv)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi\wedge\psi
iff (
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi
).

(v)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\varphi
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
iff (
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi\to\psi
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi\to\varphi
).

(vi)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
, if
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi\to\psi
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
, then
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi
.

(vii)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
, if
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
, then (
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
iff
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi
).

(viii)

For every pattern
\varphi
and every element variable
x
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\forall x.\varphi
iff
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
.

Proof.

(
i
)-(
vii
) are obtained as an immediate consequence of Proposition
3.7
.

(
viii
)
\Rightarrow
Let
e
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{A}
-valuation. Since
\mathcal{A}\vDash\forall x.\varphi
, we have that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{x\mapsto a}]

for all
a\in A
. By letting
a:=e(x)
, we get that
e=e_{x\mapsto a}
. Thus,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
.

\Leftarrow
Let
e
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{A}
-valuation and
a\in A
. We have to
prove that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{x\mapsto a}]
. This follows immediately from the fact that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
.
∎

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern. We say that

\varphi
is
valid
if
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
for every
\tau
-structure
\mathcal{A}
.

Notation:
\vDash\varphi
.

As an application of Propositions
3.11
, we get

Remark 3.13
.

(i)

\top
is valid.

(ii)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\vDash\varphi\wedge\psi
iff (
\vDash\varphi
and
\vDash\psi
).

(iii)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\vDash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
iff
(
\vDash\varphi\to\psi
and
\vDash\psi\to\varphi
).

(iv)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
, if
\vDash\varphi\to\psi
and
\vDash\varphi
, then
\vDash\psi
.

(v)

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
, if
\vDash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
, then (
\vDash\varphi
iff
\vDash\psi
).

In the sequel, we write “Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
” instead of “Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\tau
-structure
and
e
be an
\mathcal{A}
-valuation”,
“there exists
(\mathcal{A},e)
” instead of “there exists a
\tau
-structure

\mathcal{A}
and an
\mathcal{A}
-valuation
e
” and “for all
(\mathcal{A},e)
” instead of “for every

\tau
-structure
\mathcal{A}
and every
\mathcal{A}
-valuation
e
”.

3.1.1
Finite disjunctions and conjunctions

Let
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{n}(n\geq 1)
be patterns.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
. Then

\displaystyle e^{+}(\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{n})=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}e^{+}(\varphi_{i})\quad\text{and}\quad e^{+}(\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}\vee\ldots\vee\varphi_{n})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}e^{+}(\varphi_{i})

(5)

Proof.

By induction on
n
. The case
n=1
is obvious. For
n=2
apply Proposition
3.5
.(
vi
), (
v
).

n\Rightarrow n+1
: We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{n}\wedge\varphi_{n+1})

\displaystyle=e^{+}((\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{n})\wedge\varphi_{n+1})

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\varphi_{1}\wedge\varphi_{2}\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{n})\cap e^{+}(\varphi_{n+1})=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}e^{+}(\varphi_{i})\cap e^{+}(\varphi_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n+1}e^{+}(\varphi_{i}),

\displaystyle e^{+}(\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}\vee\ldots\vee\varphi_{n}\vee\varphi_{n+1})

\displaystyle=e^{+}((\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}\vee\ldots\vee\varphi_{n})\vee\varphi_{n+1})

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\varphi_{1}\vee\varphi_{2}\vee\ldots\vee\varphi_{n})\cup e^{+}(\varphi_{n+1})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}e^{+}(\varphi_{i})\cup e^{+}(\varphi_{n+1})

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n+1}e^{+}(\varphi_{i}).

∎

It follows immediately that

Proposition 3.15
.

The following hold:

(i)

For every
(\mathcal{A},e)
,

(a)

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{n})[e]
iff
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi_{i}[e]
for every
i=1,\ldots,n
.

(b)

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi_{1}\vee\ldots\vee\varphi_{n})[e]
iff
\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}e^{+}(\varphi_{i})=A
.

(ii)

For every
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{n}
iff
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi_{i}
for every
i=1,\ldots,n
.

(iii)

\vDash\varphi_{1}\wedge\ldots\wedge\varphi_{n}
iff
\vDash\varphi_{i}
for every
i=1,\ldots,n
.

3.2
Semantic consequence and equivalence

We define in the sequel three notions of semantic consequence and logic equivalence.

3.2.1
Notion 1

Definition 3.16
.

Let
\varphi
,
\psi
be patterns. We say that

(i)

\psi
is a
global semantic consequence
of
\varphi
if for every
\mathcal{A}
,

\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
implies
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi
.

Notation:
\varphi\vDash_{g}\psi
.

(ii)

\varphi
and
\psi
are
globally logically equivalent
if for every
\mathcal{A}
,

\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
iff
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi
.

Notation:
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{g}\psi
.

Remark 3.17
.

\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{g}\psi
iff (
\varphi\vDash_{g}\psi
and
\psi\vDash_{g}\varphi
).

Proof.

Obviously.∎

Remark 3.18
.

(i)

If
\varphi\vDash_{g}\psi
, then
\vDash\varphi
implies
\vDash\psi
.

(ii)

If
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{g}\psi
, then
\vDash\varphi
iff
\vDash\psi
.

Proof.

(i)

Assume that
\varphi\vDash_{g}\psi
and that
\vDash\varphi
. We have to prove that
\vDash\psi
, hence that for every
\mathcal{A}
,

\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi
. Let
\mathcal{A}
be arbitrary. Since
\vDash\varphi
, we have that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
. Since
\varphi\vDash_{g}\psi
,
it follows that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi
.

(ii)

By (
i
).

∎

3.2.2
Notion 2

Definition 3.19
.

Let
\varphi
,
\psi
be patterns. We say that

(i)

\psi
is a
local semantic consequence
of
\varphi
if for every
(\mathcal{A},e)
,

\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
implies
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
.

Notation:
\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
.

(ii)

\varphi
and
\psi
are
locally logically equivalent
if for every
(\mathcal{A},e)
,

\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
iff
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
.

Notation:
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{l}\psi
.

Remark 3.20
.

\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{l}\psi
iff (
\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
and
\psi\vDash_{l}\varphi
).

Remark 3.21
.

(i)

If
\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
, then
\vDash\varphi
implies
\vDash\psi
.

(ii)

If
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{l}\psi
, then
\vDash\varphi
iff
\vDash\psi
.

Proof.

(i)

Assume that
\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
and that
\vDash\varphi
. We have to prove that
\vDash\psi
, hence that for every
(\mathcal{A},e)
,

\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
. Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
be arbitrary. Since
\vDash\varphi
, we have that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
.
Since
\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
,
it follows that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
.

(ii)

By (
i
).

∎

3.2.3
Notion 3

Definition 3.22
.

Let
\varphi
,
\psi
be patterns. We say that

(i)

\psi
is a
strong semantic consequence
of
\varphi
if for every
(\mathcal{A},e)
,

e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
.

Notation:
\varphi\vDash_{s}\psi
.

(ii)

\varphi
and
\psi
are
strongly logically equivalent
if for every
(\mathcal{A},e)
,

e^{+}(\varphi)=e^{+}(\psi)
.

Notation:
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{s}\psi
.

Remark 3.23
.

\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{s}\psi
iff (
\varphi\vDash_{s}\psi
and
\psi\vDash_{s}\varphi
).

Remark 3.24
.

(i)

\varphi\vDash_{s}\psi
iff
\vDash\varphi\to\psi
.

(ii)

\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{s}\psi
iff
\vDash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
.

Proof.

(i)

\varphi\vDash_{s}\psi
iff for every
e
,
e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
iff for every
e
,

\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
(by Proposition
3.7
.(
ix
) iff

\vDash\varphi\to\psi
.

(ii)

\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{s}\psi
iff for every
e
,
e^{+}(\varphi)=e^{+}(\psi)
iff for every
e
,

\vDash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)[e]

(by Proposition
3.7
.(
x
)) iff
\vDash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi
.

∎

Remark 3.25
.

(i)

If
\varphi\vDash_{s}\psi
, then
\vDash\varphi
implies
\vDash\psi
.

(ii)

If
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{s}\psi
, then
\vDash\varphi
iff
\vDash\psi
.

Proof.

By Remark
3.24
and Remark
3.13
,(
iv
),(
v
).
∎

3.2.4
Relations between the three notions

Proposition 3.26
.

(i)

\varphi\vDash_{s}\psi
implies
\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
implies
\varphi\vDash_{g}\psi
.

(ii)

\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{s}\psi
implies
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{l}\psi
implies

\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{g}\psi
.

Proof.

(i)

\varphi\vDash_{s}\psi
implies
\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
: Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
be such that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
.
We have to prove that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
. As
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
, we have that

e^{+}(\varphi)=A
. Since
e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
, it follows that

e^{+}(\psi)=A
, hence
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
.

\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
implies
\varphi\vDash_{g}\psi
: Let
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
and
e
be an arbitrary

\mathcal{A}
-valuation. We have to prove that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
.
Since
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
, we have that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e]
. As
\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
, it follows that

\mathcal{A}\vDash\psi[e]
.

(ii)

Follows immediately from (
i
).

∎

Remark 3.27
.

Let
x
,
y
be element variables. Then
x\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{l}y
, hence
x\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{g}y
.

Proof.

Let
(A,e)
be arbitrary. Applying Proposition
3.7
.(
i
), we get that

(A,e)
is a model of
x
iff
\mathcal{A}\vDash x[e]
iff
|A|=1

iff
\mathcal{A}\vDash y[e]
iff
(A,e)
is a model of
y
.
Thus,
x\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{l}y
. By Proposition
3.26
.(
ii
), it follows that

x\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{g}y
.
∎

Remark 3.28
.

(i)

\varphi\vDash_{g}\psi
does not imply
\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
and
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{g}\psi
does not imply
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{l}\psi
.

(ii)

\varphi\vDash_{l}\psi
does not imply
\varphi\vDash_{s}\psi
and
\varphi\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{l}\psi
does not imply
