Then
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\psi\to\chi)[e]
, hence

e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
and
e^{+}(\psi)\subseteq e^{+}(\chi)
. It follows that

e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\chi)
, so
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\chi)[e]
.

(
51
), (
52
):
By (
15
) and
Remark
3.24
.(
ii
),
we have that
\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{s}\varphi\wedge\psi\to\chi
, hence, by
Remark
3.26
.(
ii
),

\varphi\to(\psi\to\chi)\mathrel{\text{\vtop{\halign{#\cr$\vDash$\cr\reflectbox{$\vDash$}\cr}}}}_{g}\varphi\wedge\psi\to\chi
.
Apply now
Remark
3.33
.(
ii
).

(
53
): Assume that
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi\to\psi
. Let
e
be an
\mathcal{A}
-evaluation. Then

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
, hence
e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
. We get that

e^{+}(\chi\vee\varphi)=e^{+}(\chi)\cup e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\chi)\cup e^{+}(\psi)=e^{+}(\chi\vee\psi)
. Thus,

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\chi\vee\varphi\to\chi\vee\psi)[e]
.
∎

Proposition 3.89
.

For all patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
and variables
x
,

\displaystyle\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi\to\psi

\displaystyle\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Gamma\vDash_{g}\exists x.\varphi\to\psi\quad\text{ if }x\notin FV(\psi).,

(54)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi

\displaystyle\quad\Rightarrow\quad\Gamma\vDash_{g}\forall x.\varphi.

(55)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be such that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\Gamma
.

(
54
) Assume that

\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi\to\psi
. Let
e
be an
\mathcal{A}
-evaluation. Then for every
a\in A
,

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e_{x\mapsto a}]
, so
\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)
. Since
x\notin FV(\psi)
, we have that

\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)=e^{+}(\psi)
. Thus, we have got that for every
a\in A
,
\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
. It follows that

e^{+}(\exists x.\varphi)=\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
, that is
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\exists x.\varphi\to\psi)[e]
.

(
55
) Assume that
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi
. Let
e
be an
\mathcal{A}
-evaluation. Then for every
a\in A
,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{x\mapsto a}]
, as

\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
. Thus,
\mathcal{A}\vDash\forall x\varphi
.
∎

3.14
Application

Proposition 3.90
.

For all patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\chi
,

\displaystyle\vDash{\varphi}\cdot{\bot}\leftrightarrow\bot,

(56)

\displaystyle\vDash{\bot}\cdot{\varphi}\leftrightarrow\bot,

(57)

\displaystyle\vDash{(\varphi\vee\psi)}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\vee{\psi}\cdot{\chi},

(58)

\displaystyle\vDash{\chi}\cdot{(\varphi\vee\psi)}\leftrightarrow{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\vee{\chi}\cdot{\psi},

(59)

\displaystyle\vDash{(\exists x.\varphi)}\cdot{\psi}\leftrightarrow\exists x.{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}\quad\text{ if }x\notin FV(\psi),

(60)

\displaystyle\vDash{\psi}\cdot{(\exists x.\varphi)}\leftrightarrow{\exists x.\psi}\cdot{\varphi}\quad\text{ if }x\not\in FV(\psi).

(61)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
.

(
56
), (
57
): Apply (
163
) to get that

e^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\bot})=e^{+}(\varphi)\star e^{+}(\bot)=e^{+}(\varphi)\star\emptyset=\emptyset=e^{+}(\bot)
and
e^{+}({\bot}\cdot{\varphi})=e^{+}(\bot)\star e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset\star e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset=e^{+}(\bot)
.

(
58
): We have that
e^{+}({(\varphi\vee\psi)}\cdot{\chi})=e^{+}(\varphi\vee\psi)\star e^{+}(\chi)=(e^{+}(\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\psi))\star e^{+}(\chi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{C-cup-D-appls-B}}}{{=}}(e^{+}(\varphi)\star e^{+}(\chi))\cup(e^{+}(\psi)\star e^{+}(\chi))=e^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\chi})\cup e^{+}({\psi}\cdot{\chi})=e^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\vee{\psi}\cdot{\chi})
.

(
59
): We have that
e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{(\varphi\vee\psi)})=e^{+}(\chi)\star e^{+}(\varphi\vee\psi)=e^{+}(\chi)\star(e^{+}(\varphi)\cup e^{+}(\psi))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-appls-C-cup-D}}}{{=}}(e^{+}(\chi)\star e^{+}(\varphi))\cup(e^{+}(\chi)\star e^{+}(\psi))=e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\varphi})\cup e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\psi})=e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\vee{\chi}\cdot{\psi})
.

(
60
): We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}({(\exists x.\varphi)}\cdot{\psi})

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\exists x.\varphi)\star e^{+}(\psi)=\left(\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\right)\star e^{+}(\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{cup-family-appls-B}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{a\in A}(\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\star e^{+}(\psi))

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\star\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)\right)\quad\text{as }x\notin FV(\psi),\text{ hence }\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)=e^{+}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})=e^{+}(\exists x.{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}).

(
61
): We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}({\psi}\cdot{(\exists x.\varphi)})

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\psi)\star e^{+}(\exists x.\varphi)=e^{+}(\psi)\star\left(\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-appls-cup-family}}}{{=}}\bigcup_{a\in A}(e^{+}(\psi)\star\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi))

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)\star\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\right)\quad\text{as }x\notin FV(\psi),\text{ hence }\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)=e^{+}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}({\psi}\cdot{\varphi})=e^{+}(\exists x.{\psi}\cdot{\varphi}).

∎

Proposition 3.91
.

For all patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\chi
,

\displaystyle\vDash{(\varphi\wedge\psi)}\cdot{\chi}\to{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\wedge{\psi}\cdot{\chi},

(62)

\displaystyle\vDash{\chi}\cdot{(\varphi\wedge\psi)}\to{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\wedge{\chi}\cdot{\psi},

(63)

\displaystyle\vDash{(\forall x.\varphi)}\cdot{\psi}\to\forall x.{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}\quad\text{ if }x\notin FV(\psi),

(64)

\displaystyle\vDash{\psi}\cdot{(\forall x.\varphi)}\to{\forall x.\psi}\cdot{\varphi}\quad\text{ if }x\not\in FV(\psi).

(65)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
.

(
62
): We have that
e^{+}({(\varphi\wedge\psi)}\cdot{\chi})=e^{+}(\varphi\wedge\psi)\star e^{+}(\chi)=(e^{+}(\varphi)\cap e^{+}(\psi))\star e^{+}(\chi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{C-cap-D-appls-B}}}{{\subseteq}}(e^{+}(\varphi)\star e^{+}(\chi))\cap(e^{+}(\psi)\star e^{+}(\chi))=e^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\chi})\cap e^{+}({\psi}\cdot{\chi})=e^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\wedge{\psi}\cdot{\chi})
.

(
63
): We have that
e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{(\varphi\wedge\psi)})=e^{+}(\chi)\star e^{+}(\varphi\wedge\psi)=e^{+}(\chi)\star(e^{+}(\varphi)\cap e^{+}(\psi))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-appls-C-cap-D}}}{{\subseteq}}(e^{+}(\chi)\star e^{+}(\varphi))\cap(e^{+}(\chi)\star e^{+}(\psi))=e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\varphi})\cap e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\psi})=e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\wedge{\chi}\cdot{\psi})
.

(
64
): We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}({(\forall x.\varphi)}\cdot{\psi})

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\forall x.\varphi)\star e^{+}(\psi)=\left(\bigcap_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\right)\star e^{+}(\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{cap-family-appls-B}}}{{\subseteq}}\bigcap_{a\in A}(\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\star e^{+}(\psi))

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{a\in A}\left(\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\star\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)\right)\quad\text{as }x\notin FV(\psi),\text{ hence }\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)=e^{+}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\psi})=e^{+}(\forall x.{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}).

(
65
):

\displaystyle e^{+}({\psi}\cdot{(\forall x.\varphi)})

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\psi)\star e^{+}(\forall x.\varphi)=e^{+}(\psi)\star\left(\bigcap_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-appls-cap-family}}}{{\subseteq}}\bigcap_{a\in A}(e^{+}(\psi)\star\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi))

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{a\in A}\left(\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)\star\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\right)\quad\text{as }x\notin FV(\psi),\text{ hence }\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(\psi)=e^{+}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}({\psi}\cdot{\varphi})=e^{+}(\forall x.{\psi}\cdot{\varphi}).

∎

3.14.1
\vDash_{l}

Proposition 3.92
.

For all patterns
\varphi
,

,
\chi
,

\displaystyle\varphi\to\psi\,\,\vDash_{l}

\displaystyle\,\,{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\psi},

(66)

\displaystyle\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\,\,\vDash_{l}

\displaystyle\,\,{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\leftrightarrow{\chi}\cdot{\psi},

(67)

\displaystyle\varphi\to\psi\,\,\vDash_{l}

\displaystyle\,\,{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi},

(68)

\displaystyle\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\,\,\vDash_{l}

\displaystyle\,\,{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\chi},

\displaystyle\varphi\to\psi\,\,\vDash_{l}

\displaystyle\,\,{\chi}\cdot{\neg\psi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\neg\varphi},

(70)

\displaystyle\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\,\,\vDash_{l}

\displaystyle\,\,{\chi}\cdot{\neg\psi}\leftrightarrow{\chi}\cdot{\neg\varphi},

\displaystyle\,\,{\neg\psi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\neg\varphi}\cdot{\chi},

(72)

\displaystyle\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\,\,\vDash_{l}

\displaystyle\,\,{\neg\psi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\neg\varphi}\cdot{\chi}.

(73)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
.

(
66
): Assume that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
,
hence
e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
. It follows that

e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\varphi})=e^{+}(\chi)\star e^{+}(\varphi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-se-C-appls-D}}}{{\subseteq}}e^{+}(\chi)\star e^{+}(\psi)=e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\psi})
, hence
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\psi})[e]
.

(
67
): Assume that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)[e]
. Then, by Proposition
3.7
.(
xi
),

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\psi\to\varphi)[e]
. Apply now (
66
) to get that

\mathcal{A}\vDash({\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\psi})[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\chi}\cdot{\psi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\varphi})[e]
, hence, by applying again
Proposition
3.7
.(
xi
),
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\leftrightarrow{\chi}\cdot{\psi})[e]

(
68
): Assume that

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
, hence
e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
. It follows that

e^{+}({\varphi}\cdot{\chi})=e^{+}(\varphi)\star e^{+}(\chi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-se-C-appls-D}}}{{\subseteq}}e^{+}(\psi)\star e^{+}(\chi)=e^{+}({\psi}\cdot{\chi})
, hence
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi})[e]
.

(
69
): Assume that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)[e]
. Then, by Proposition
3.7
.(
xi
),

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\psi\to\varphi)[e]
. Apply now (
68
) to get that

\mathcal{A}\vDash({\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi})[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\psi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\varphi}\cdot{\chi})[e]
, hence, by applying again
Proposition
3.7
.(
xi
),
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\chi})[e]
.

(
70
): Assume that

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
, hence
e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
. It follows that

e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\neg\psi})=e^{+}(\chi)\star e^{+}(\neg\psi)=e^{+}(\chi)\star C_{A}e^{+}(\psi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-se-C-appls-D-comp}}}{{\subseteq}}e^{+}(\chi)\star C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)=e^{+}({\chi}\cdot{\neg\varphi})
, hence
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\chi}\cdot{\neg\psi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\neg\varphi})[e]
.

(
71
): Assume that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)[e]
. Then, by Proposition
3.7
.(
xi
),

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\psi\to\varphi)[e]
. Apply now (
70
) to get that

\mathcal{A}\vDash({\chi}\cdot{\neg\psi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\neg\varphi})[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\chi}\cdot{\neg\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\neg\psi})[e]
, hence, by applying again

3.7
.(

),
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\chi}\cdot{\neg\psi}\leftrightarrow{\chi}\cdot{\neg\varphi})[e]
.

(
72
): Assume that

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
, hence
e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
. It follows that

e^{+}({\neg\psi}\cdot{\chi})=e^{+}(\neg\psi)\star e^{+}(\chi)=C_{A}e^{+}(\psi)\star e^{+}(\chi)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-se-C-appls-D-comp}}}{{\subseteq}}C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)\star e^{+}(\chi)=e^{+}(\neg\varphi)\star e^{+}(\chi)=e^{+}({\neg\varphi}\cdot{\chi})
, hence
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\neg\psi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\neg\varphi}\cdot{\chi})[e]
.

(
73
): Assume that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)[e]
. Then, by Proposition
3.7
.(
xi
),

\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\psi\to\varphi)[e]
. Apply now (

) to get that

\mathcal{A}\vDash({\neg\psi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\neg\varphi}\cdot{\chi})[e]
and
\mathcal{A}\vDash({\neg\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\neg\psi}\cdot{\chi})[e]
, hence, by applying again

.(

.
∎

3.15
Contexts

Proposition 3.100
.

Let
C
be a context,
\varphi
be a pattern, and
(\mathcal{A},e)
.

\displaystyle e^{+}(C[\varphi])

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}e^{+}(\varphi)&\text{if~}C=\Box,\\
e^{+}(C_{1}[\varphi])\star e^{+}(\psi)&\text{if~}C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\psi,\\
e^{+}(\psi)\star e^{+}(C_{1}[\varphi])&\text{if~}Appl_{\Box}\psi C_{1}.\end{cases}

(106)

Proof.

Apply Proposition
2.57
. We have the following cases:

(i)

C=\Box
. Then
C[\varphi]=\varphi
, so
e^{+}(C[\varphi])=e^{+}(\varphi)
.

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\psi
. Then
C[\varphi]={C_{1}[\varphi]}\cdot{\psi}
, hence

e^{+}(C[\varphi])=e^{+}(C_{1}[\varphi])\star e^{+}(\psi)
.

(iii)

C=Appl_{\Box}\psi C_{1}
. Then
C[\varphi]={\psi}\cdot{C_{1}[\varphi]}
, hence

e^{+}(C[\varphi])=e^{+}(\psi)\star e^{+}(C_{1}[\varphi])
.

∎

Proposition 3.101
.

Let
C
be a context and
\varphi
,
\psi
be patterns.

\displaystyle\vDash C[\bot]\leftrightarrow\bot,

(107)

\displaystyle\vDash C[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow C[\varphi]\vee C[\psi],

(108)

\displaystyle\vDash C[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow\exists x.C[\varphi]\quad\text{ if }x\notin FV(C),

(109)

\displaystyle\vDash C[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to C[\varphi]\wedge C[\psi],

(110)

\displaystyle\vDash C[\forall x.\varphi]\to\forall x.C[\varphi]\quad\text{ if }x\notin FV(C).

(111)

Proof.

(
107
): The proof is by induction on contexts:

(i)

C=\Box
. Then
C[\bot]=\bot
. Apply (
29
).

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\psi
and, by the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}
,
\vDash C_{1}[\bot]\leftrightarrow\bot
. Since

C[\bot]={C_{1}[\bot]}\cdot{\psi}
, we can apply (
89
) to get that

C_{1}[\bot]\leftrightarrow\bot\vDash_{g}C[\bot]\leftrightarrow{\bot}\cdot{\psi}
and Remark
3.18
.(
i
) to get that

\vDash C[\bot]\leftrightarrow{\bot}\cdot{\psi}
. Hence, by (
57
) and (
31
), it follows that

\vDash C[\bot]\leftrightarrow\bot
.

(iii)

C=Appl_{\Box}\psi C_{1}
and, by the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}
,
\vDash C_{1}[\bot]\leftrightarrow\bot
. Since

C[\bot]={\psi}\cdot{C_{1}[\bot]}
, we can apply (
87
) to get that
C_{1}[\bot]\leftrightarrow\bot\vDash_{g}C[\bot]\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\bot}

and Remark
3.18
.(
i
) to get that

\vDash C[\bot]\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{\bot}
. Hence, by (
56
) and (
31
), it follows that

\vDash C[\bot]\leftrightarrow\bot
.

(
108
): The proof is by induction on contexts:

(i)

C=\Box
. Then
C[\varphi\vee\psi]=\varphi\vee\psi=C[\varphi]\vee C[\psi]
. Apply (
29
).

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\chi
and, by the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}
,

\vDash C_{1}[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow C_{1}[\varphi]\vee C_{1}[\psi]
.
Since

C[\varphi\vee\psi]={C_{1}[\varphi\vee\psi]}\cdot{\chi}
, we can apply (
89
) to get that

C_{1}[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow C_{1}[\varphi]\vee C_{1}[\psi]\vDash_{g}C[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow{(C_{1}[\varphi]\vee C_{1}[\psi])}\cdot{\chi}

and Remark
3.18
.(
i
) to get that

\vDash C[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow{(C_{1}[\varphi]\vee C_{1}[\psi])}\cdot{\chi}
.
Hence, by (
58
) and (
31
), it follows that

\vDash C[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow{C_{1}[\varphi]}\cdot{\chi}\vee{C_{2}[\varphi]}\cdot{\chi}=C[\varphi]\vee C[\psi]
.

(iii)

C=Appl_{\Box}\chi C_{1}
and, by the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}
,

\vDash C_{1}[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow C_{1}[\varphi]\vee C_{1}[\psi]
.
Since

C[\varphi\vee\psi]={\chi}\cdot{C_{1}[\varphi\vee\psi]}
, we can apply (
87
) to get that

C_{1}[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow C_{1}[\varphi]\vee C_{1}[\psi]\vDash_{g}C[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow{\chi}\cdot{(C_{1}[\varphi]\vee C_{1}[\psi])}

and Remark
3.18
.(
i
) to get that

\vDash C[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow{\chi}\cdot{(C_{1}[\varphi]\vee C_{1}[\psi])}
.
Hence, by (
59
) and (
31
), it follows that

\vDash C[\varphi\vee\psi]\leftrightarrow{\chi}\cdot{C_{1}[\varphi]}\vee{\chi}\cdot{C_{2}[\varphi]}=C[\varphi]\vee C[\psi]
.

(
109
): The proof is by induction on contexts:

(i)

C=\Box
. Then
C[\exists x.\varphi]=\exists x.\varphi=\exists x.C[\varphi]
. Apply (
29
).

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\psi
. As
x\notin FV(C)
and, by Definition
2.62
,

FV(C)=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(\varphi)
,
we have that
x\notin FV(C_{1})
, so we can apply the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}

to get that
\vDash C_{1}[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow\exists x.C_{1}[\varphi]
.
Since
C[\exists x.\varphi]={C_{1}[\exists x.\varphi]}\cdot{\psi}
, we can apply
(
89
) to get that

C_{1}[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow\exists x.C_{1}[\varphi]\vDash_{g}C[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow{(\exists x.C_{1}[\varphi])}\cdot{\psi}

and Remark
3.18
.(
i
) to get that

\vDash C[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow{(\exists x.C_{1}[\varphi])}\cdot{\psi}
.
Hence, by (
60
) and (
31
), it follows that

\vDash C[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow\exists x.{C_{1}[\varphi]}\cdot{\psi}=\exists x.C[\varphi]
.

(iii)

C=Appl_{\Box}\psi C_{1}
. As
x\notin FV(C)
and, by Definition
2.62
,

FV(C)=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(\varphi)
,
we have that
x\notin FV(C_{1})
, so we can apply the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}

to get that
\vDash C_{1}[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow\exists x.C_{1}[\varphi]
.
Since
C[\exists x.\varphi]={\psi}\cdot{C_{1}[\exists x.\varphi]}
, we can apply
(
87
) to get that

C_{1}[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow\exists x.C_{1}[\varphi]\vDash_{g}C[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{(\exists x.C_{1}[\varphi])}

and Remark
3.18
.(
i
) to get that

\vDash C[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow{\psi}\cdot{(\exists x.C_{1}[\varphi])}
.
Hence, by (
61
) and (
31
), it follows that

\vDash C[\exists x.\varphi]\leftrightarrow\exists x.{\psi}\cdot{C_{1}[\varphi]}=\exists x.C[\varphi]
.

(
110
): The proof is by induction on contexts:

(i)

C=\Box
. Then
C[\varphi\wedge\psi]=\varphi\wedge\psi=C[\varphi]\wedge C[\psi]
. Apply (
29
) and
Remark
3.13
.(
iii
).

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\chi
and, by the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}
,

\vDash C_{1}[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to C_{1}[\varphi]\wedge C_{1}[\psi]
.
Since

C[\varphi\wedge\psi]={C_{1}[\varphi\wedge\psi]}\cdot{\chi}
, we can apply (
88
) to get that

C_{1}[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to C_{1}[\varphi]\wedge C_{1}[\psi]\vDash_{g}C[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to{(C_{1}[\varphi]\wedge C_{1}[\psi])}\cdot{\chi}

and Remark
3.18
.(
i
)
to get that

\vDash C[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to{(C_{1}[\varphi]\wedge C_{1}[\psi])}\cdot{\chi}
.
Hence, by (
62
) and (
32
), it follows that

\vDash C[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to{C_{1}[\varphi]}\cdot{\chi}\wedge{C_{2}[\psi]}\cdot{\chi}=C[\varphi]\wedge C[\psi]
.

(iii)

C=Appl_{\Box}\chi C_{1}
and, by the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}
,

\vDash C_{1}[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to C_{1}[\varphi]\wedge C_{1}[\psi]
.
Since

C[\varphi\wedge\psi]={\chi}\cdot{C_{1}[\varphi\wedge\psi]}
, we can apply (
86
)
to get that

C_{1}[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to C_{1}[\varphi]\wedge C_{1}[\psi]\vDash_{g}C[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to{\chi}\cdot{(C_{1}[\varphi]\wedge C_{1}[\psi])}

and Remark
3.18
.(
i
)
to get that

\vDash C[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to{\chi}\cdot{(C_{1}[\varphi]\wedge C_{1}[\psi])}
.
Hence, by (
63
) and (
32
), it follows that

\vDash C[\varphi\wedge\psi]\to{\chi}\cdot{C_{1}[\varphi]}\wedge{\chi}\cdot{C_{2}[\psi]}=C[\varphi]\wedge C[\psi]
.

(
111
): The proof is by induction on contexts:

(i)

C=\Box
. Then
C[\forall x.\varphi]=\forall x.\varphi=\forall x.C[\varphi]
. Apply (
29
) and
Remark
3.13
.(
iii
).

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\psi
. As
x\notin FV(C)
and, by Definition
2.62
,

FV(C)=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(\varphi)
,
we have that
x\notin FV(C_{1})
, so we can apply the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}

to get that
\vDash C_{1}[\forall x.\varphi]\to\forall x.C_{1}[\varphi]
.
Since
C[\forall x.\varphi]={C_{1}[\forall x.\varphi]}\cdot{\psi}
, we can apply
(
88
) to get that

C_{1}[\forall x.\varphi]\to\forall x.C_{1}[\varphi]\vDash_{g}C[\forall x.\varphi]\to{(\forall x.C_{1}[\varphi])}\cdot{\psi}

and Remark
3.18
.(
i
) to get that

\vDash C[\forall x.\varphi]\to{(\forall x.C_{1}[\varphi])}\cdot{\psi}
.
Hence, by (
64
) and (
32
), it follows that

\vDash C[\forall x.\varphi]\to\forall x.{C_{1}[\varphi]}\cdot{\psi}=\forall x.C[\varphi]
.

(iii)

C=Appl_{\Box}\psi C_{1}
. As
x\notin FV(C)
and, by Definition
2.62
,

FV(C)=FV(C_{1})\cup FV(\varphi)
,
we have that
x\notin FV(C_{1})
, so we can apply the induction hypothesis for
C_{1}

to get that
\vDash C_{1}[\forall x.\varphi]\to\forall x.C_{1}[\varphi]
.
Since
C[\forall x.\varphi]={\psi}\cdot{C_{1}[\forall x.\varphi]}
, we can apply
(
86
) to get that

C_{1}[\forall x.\varphi]\to\forall x.C_{1}[\varphi]\vDash_{g}C[\forall x.\varphi]\to{\psi}\cdot{(\forall x.C_{1}[\varphi])}

and
Remark
3.18
.(
i
) to get that

\vDash C[\forall x.\varphi]\to{\psi}\cdot{(\forall x.C_{1}[\varphi])}
.
Hence, by (
65
) and (
32
), it follows that

\vDash C[\forall x.\varphi]\to\forall x.{\psi}\cdot{C_{1}[\varphi]}=\forall x.C[\varphi]
.

∎

3.15.1
\vDash_{l}

Proposition 3.102
.

Let
C
be a context. Then for every patterns,
\varphi
,
\psi
,

\displaystyle\varphi\to\psi\,\,\vDash_{l}

\displaystyle\,\,C[\varphi]\to C[\psi],

(112)

\displaystyle\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\,\,\vDash_{l}

\displaystyle\,\,C[\varphi]\leftrightarrow C[\psi].

(113)

Proof.

(
112
): Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
be such that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\psi)[e]
. We prove that

\mathcal{A}\vDash(C[\varphi]\to C[\psi])[e]
by induction on
C
.

(i)

C=\Box
. Then
C[\varphi]\to C[\psi]=\varphi\to\psi
and the conclusion is obvious.

(ii)

C=Appl_{\Box}C_{1}\chi
. By the induction hypothesis,
we have that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(C_{1}[\varphi]\to C_{1}[\psi])[e]
. Apply now (
68
) to get that

\mathcal{A}\vDash({C_{1}[\varphi]}\cdot{\chi}\to{C_{1}[\psi]}\cdot{\chi})[e]
, that is
\mathcal{A}\vDash(C[\varphi]\to C[\psi])[e]
.

(iii)

C=Appl_{\Box}\chi C_{1}
. By the induction hypothesis,
we have that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(C_{1}[\varphi]\to C_{1}[\psi])[e]
. Apply now (
66
) to get that

\mathcal{A}\vDash({\chi}\cdot{C_{1}[\varphi]}\to{\chi}\cdot{C_{1}[\psi]})[e]
, that is
\mathcal{A}\vDash(C[\varphi]\to C[\psi])[e]
.

(
113
): Apply (
112
) and
Proposition
3.7
.(
xi
).
∎

Proposition 3.103
.

Let
C
be a context,
\Gamma
be a set of patterns and
\varphi
,
\psi
be patterns. Then

\displaystyle\Gamma\vDash_{l}\varphi\to\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vDash_{l}C[\varphi]\to C[\psi],

(114)

\displaystyle\Gamma\vDash_{l}\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vDash_{l}C[\varphi]\leftrightarrow C[\psi].

(115)

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.102
and
Lemma
3.40
.(
i
).
∎

3.15.3
(Singleton Variable)

Lemma 3.106
.

Let
C
be a context and
\varphi
be a pattern. Then for every
(\mathcal{A},e)
,

e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset\quad\text{implies}\quad e^{+}(C[\varphi])=\emptyset.

Proof.

Since
e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset=e^{+}(\bot)
, we have that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\bot)[e]
. Apply
(
113
) to get that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(C[\varphi]\leftrightarrow C[\bot])[e]
, so
e^{+}(C[\varphi])=e^{+}(C[\bot])
.
As, by (
107
),
\vDash C[\bot]\leftrightarrow\bot
, it follows that
e^{+}(C[\bot])=e^{+}(\bot)=\emptyset
. Thus,
we have got that
e^{+}(C[\varphi])=\emptyset
.
∎

Proposition 3.107
.

For every contexts
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, pattern
\varphi
and element variable
x
,

\displaystyle\vDash\neg(C_{1}[x\wedge\varphi]\wedge C_{2}[x\wedge\neg\varphi]).

(120)

Proof.

Denote
\theta=C_{1}[x\wedge\varphi]\wedge C_{2}[x\wedge\neg\varphi]
.
Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
. We have to prove that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\neg\theta)[e]
, that is

e^{+}(\neg\theta)=A
, hence that
e^{+}(\theta)=\emptyset
.
We have two cases:

(i)

e(x)\in e^{+}(\varphi)
. Then

e^{+}(x\wedge\neg\varphi)=e^{+}(x)\cap e^{+}(\neg\varphi)=\{e(x)\}\cap C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset
.
Apply Lemma
3.106
to get that
e^{+}(C_{2}[x\wedge\neg\varphi])=\emptyset
. It follows that

e^{+}(\theta)=e^{+}(C_{1}[x\wedge\varphi])\cap e^{+}(C_{2}[x\wedge\neg\varphi])=e^{+}(C_{1}[x\wedge\varphi])\cap\emptyset=\emptyset
.

(ii)

e(x)\notin e^{+}(\varphi)
. Then

e^{+}(x\wedge\varphi)=\{e(x)\}\cap e^{+}(\varphi)=e^{+}(x)\cap e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset
.
Apply Lemma
3.106
to get that
e^{+}(C_{1}[x\wedge\varphi])=\emptyset
. It follows that

e^{+}(\theta)=e^{+}(C_{1}[x\wedge\varphi])\cap e^{+}(C_{2}[x\wedge\neg\varphi])=\emptyset\cap e^{+}(C_{2}[x\wedge\neg\varphi])=\emptyset
.

∎

3.16
Set variables

Let
\Gamma
be a set of patterns.

3.16.1
\vDash_{g}

Proposition 3.108
.

Let
\varphi,\psi
be patterns and
X
be a set variable
such that
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi
. Then

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\vDash_{g}{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a model of
\varphi
and
e
be an arbitrary
\mathcal{A}
-evaluation. We have to prove that

\mathcal{A}\vDash{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}[e]
. By Proposition
3.76
, we
have that
e^{+}({Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}})=\left(e_{X\mapsto{e^{+}(\psi)}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)
.
Since
\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi
, we have in particular that

\mathcal{A}\vDash\varphi[e_{X\mapsto{e^{+}(\psi)}}]
. Thus,
\mathcal{A}\vDash{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}[e]
.
∎

Proposition 3.109
.

Let
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi,\psi\}
be a set of patterns and
X
be a set variable
such that
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi
. Then

\displaystyle\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vDash_{g}{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.108
and
Lemma
3.33
.(
i
).
∎

Proposition 3.110
.

For all patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
and any set variable
X
,

\displaystyle\varphi

\displaystyle\vDash_{g}\varphi_{X}({\psi}).

Proof.

We have the following two cases:

(i)

X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi
. Then
\varphi_{X}({\psi})={Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}
, so we apply
Proposition
3.108
to get the conclusion.

(ii)

X
is not free for
\psi
in
\varphi
. Then
\varphi_{X}({\psi})={Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\theta}}
with

\theta=Subb^{U_{1}}_{Z_{1}}Subb^{U_{2}}_{Z_{2}}\ldots Subb^{U_{p}}_{Z_{p}}Subb^{u_{1}}_{z_{1}}Subb^{u_{2}}_{z_{2}}\ldots Subb^{u_{k}}_{z_{k}}\varphi,

where

(a)

u_{1},\ldots,u_{k}
are the element variables and
U_{1},\ldots,U_{p}
are the set variables that occur bound in
\varphi
and also
occur in
\psi
;

(b)

z_{1},\ldots,z_{k}
are new element variables and
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{p}
are
new set variables, that do not occur in
\varphi
or
\psi
.

Applying repeatedly Propositions
3.74
,
3.78
, we get that

\vDash\theta\leftrightarrow\varphi.

(121)

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a model of
\varphi
. By (
121
), we get that
\mathcal{A}\vDash\theta
.
Apply Proposition
3.108
(with
\varphi=\theta
) to get that

\mathcal{A}\vDash{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\theta}}=\varphi_{X}({\psi})
.

∎

Proposition 3.111
.

For all patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,

\displaystyle\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi

\displaystyle\quad\text{implies}\quad\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi_{X}({\psi}).

Proof.

Apply Proposition
3.110
and
Lemma
3.33
.(
i
).
∎

3.17
\mu

Proposition 3.112
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern and
X
be a set variable such that
\varphi
is positive in
X
and
X
is free for
\mu X.\varphi
in
\varphi
. Then

\displaystyle\vDash{Subf^{X}_{\mu X.\varphi}{\varphi}}\leftrightarrow\mu X.\varphi.

(122)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
. We have that

\displaystyle e^{+}\left({Subf^{X}_{\mu X.\varphi}{\varphi}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(e_{X\mapsto{e^{+}(\mu X.\varphi)}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\quad\text{by Proposition~\ref{eval-subfvpXdelta}}

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\mu X.\varphi)\quad\text{by Proposition~\ref{fkntar-fp}.\eqref{fkntar-mu-nu-fp}}

∎

3.17.1
\vDash_{l}

Proposition 3.113
.

Let
\varphi,\psi
be patterns and
X
be a set variable
such that
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi
. Then

\displaystyle{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\to\psi

\displaystyle\vDash_{l}\mu X.\varphi\to\psi.

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
be such that
\mathcal{A}\vDash({Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\to\psi)[e]
, so

e^{+}\left({Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\right)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
.
By Propositions
3.76
, we have that

e^{+}\left({Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\right)=\left(e_{X\mapsto{e^{+}(\psi)}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)
. We get that

\left(e_{X\mapsto{e^{+}(\psi)}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\psi)
, hence

e^{+}(\psi)\in\{B\subseteq A\mid\left(e_{X\mapsto{B}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq B\}
. It follows
that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\mu X.\varphi)=\bigcap\{B\subseteq A\mid\left(e_{X\mapsto{B}}\right)^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq B\}\subseteq e^{+}(\psi),

so
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\mu X.\varphi\to\psi)[e]
.
∎

.(
i
).

4
Proof system

Let
\tau=(EVar,SVar,\Sigma)
be a signature.

We present in the sequel a Hilbert type proof system for AML, that we denote
\mathcal{P}
, obtained
by combining the ones from
[
4
]
and
[
1
]
.
\mathcal{P}
is also given in Section
A
.

Definition 4.1
.

The
axioms
of
\mathcal{P}
are the following patterns

(Tautology)

\varphi
if
\varphi
is a tautology

(
\exists
-Quantifier)

Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\exists x.\varphi
if
x
is free
for
y
in
\varphi

(Propagation
⊥
)

{\varphi}\cdot{\bot}\to\bot

{\bot}\cdot{\varphi}\to\bot

(Propagation
∨
)

{(\varphi\vee\psi)}\cdot{\chi}\to{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\vee{\psi}\cdot{\chi}

{\chi}\cdot{(\varphi\vee\psi)}\to{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\vee{\chi}\cdot{\psi}

(Propagation
∃
)

{(\exists x.\varphi)}\cdot{\psi}\to\exists x.{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}
,
{\psi}\cdot{(\exists x.\varphi)}\to{\exists x.\psi}\cdot{\varphi}

if
x\not\in FV(\psi)

(Pre-Fixpoint)

{Subf^{X}_{\mu X.\varphi}{\varphi}}\to\mu X.\varphi
if
\varphi
is positive in
X

and
X
is free for
\mu X.\varphi
in
\varphi

(existence)

\exists x.x
,

(Singleton Variable)

\neg(C_{1}[x\wedge\varphi]\wedge C_{2}[x\wedge\neg\varphi])
.

Definition 4.2
.

The
deduction rules
(or
inference rules
) of
\mathcal{P}
are the following:

(modus ponens)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi\quad\varphi\to\psi}{\psi}

(
\exists
-quantifier rule)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi\to\psi}{\exists x.\varphi\to\psi}
if
x\not\in FV(\psi)

(framing)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi\to\psi}{{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi}}\qquad\frac{\varphi\to\psi}{{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\psi}}

(Set Variable Substitution)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi}{{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}}
if
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi

(Knaster-Tarski)

\displaystyle\frac{{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\to\psi}{\mu X.\varphi\to\psi}
if
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi

Thus, we have two types of inference rules:

(i)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi}{\chi}
.
This inference rule is read as follows: from
\varphi
infer
\chi
.
\varphi
is said to be the
premise
of the rule and
\chi
is the

conclusion
of the rule.

(ii)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi\quad\psi}{\chi}
.
This inference rule is read as follows: from
\varphi
and
\psi
infer
\chi
.
\varphi
and
\psi
are said to be the
premise
of the rule and
\chi
is the

conclusion
of the rule.

Let
\Gamma
be a set of patterns.

Definition 4.3
.

The
\Gamma
-theorems
are the patterns inductively defined as follows:

(i)

Every axiom is a
\Gamma
-theorem.

(ii)

Every pattern of
\Gamma
is a
\Gamma
-theorem.

(iii)

For every inference rule, the following holds: If its premises are
\Gamma
-theorems, then its conclusion
is a
\Gamma
-theorem.

(iv)

Only the patterns obtained by applying the above rules are
\Gamma
-theorems.

Definition 4.4
.

[Alternative definition for
\Gamma
-theorems ]

The set of
\Gamma
-theorems
is the intersection of all
sets
\Delta
of patterns that have the following properties:

(i)

\Delta
contains all the axioms.

(ii)

\Delta
contains all the patterns from
\Gamma
, that is
\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
.

(iii)

For every inference rule, the following holds: If
\Delta
contains its premise(s), then its conclusion is also in
\Delta
.

The set of
\Gamma
-theorems is denoted by
Thm(\Gamma)
. If
\varphi
is a
\Gamma
-theorem, then we also say that
\varphi
is
deduced from the hypotheses

\Gamma
.

As an immediate consequence of Definition
4.4
, we get the induction principle for
\Gamma
-theorems.

Proposition 4.5
.

[Induction principle on
\Gamma
-theorems ]

Let
\Delta
be a set of patterns satisfying the following properties:

(i)

\Delta
contains all the axioms.

(ii)

\Gamma\subseteq\Delta
.

(iii)

For every inference rule, the following holds: If
\Delta
contains its premise(s), then its conclusion is also in
\Delta
.

Then
Thm(\Gamma)\subseteq\Delta
.

Notation 4.6
.

Let
\Gamma,\Delta
be sets of patterns and
\varphi
be a pattern. We use the following notations

\Gamma\vdash\varphi

=

\varphi
is a
\Gamma
-theorem.

\vdash\varphi

=

\emptyset\vdash\varphi
,

\Gamma\vdash\Delta

\Leftrightarrow

\Gamma\vdash\varphi
for any
\varphi\in\Delta
.

Definition 4.7
.

A pattern
\varphi
is called a
theorem
if
\vdash\varphi
.

Proposition 4.8
.

Let
\Gamma,\Delta
be sets of patterns.

(i)

Assume that
\Delta\subseteq\Gamma
. Then for every pattern
\varphi
,
Thm(\Delta)\subseteq Thm(\Gamma)
, that is

\Delta\vdash\varphi
implies
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
.

(ii)

For every pattern
\varphi
,
Thm(\emptyset)\subseteq Thm(\Gamma)
, that is

\vdash\varphi
implies
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
.

(iii)

Assume that
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
. Then for every pattern
\varphi
,
Thm(\Delta)\subseteq Thm(\Gamma)
, that is

\Delta\vdash\varphi
implies

\Gamma\vdash\varphi
.

(iv)

For every pattern
\varphi
,
Thm(Thm(\Gamma))=Thm(\Gamma)
, that is

Thm(\Gamma)\vdash\varphi
iff
\Gamma\vdash\varphi
.

Proof.

(i)

As
\Delta\subseteq\Gamma
, one proves immediately by induction on
\Delta
-theorems that
Thm(\Delta)\subseteq Thm(\Gamma)
.

(ii)

Apply (
i
) with
\Delta=\emptyset
.

(iii)

As, by hypothesis,
\Delta\subseteq Thm(\Gamma)
, one proves immediately by induction on
\Delta
-theorems that

Thm(\Delta)\subseteq Thm(\Gamma)
.

(iv)

\Leftarrow
As, by definition,
\Gamma\subseteq Thm(\Gamma)
, we can apply (
i
) to get that

Thm(\Gamma)\subseteq Thm(Thm(\Gamma))
.

\Rightarrow
We have that
\Gamma\vdash Thm(\Gamma)
, so we can apply (
iii
) with
\Delta=Thm(\Gamma)

to get that
Thm(Thm(\Gamma))\subseteq Thm(\Gamma)
.

∎

4.1
\Gamma
-proofs

Definition 4.9
.

A
\Gamma
-proof
(or
proof from the hypotheses
\Gamma
) is
a sequence of patterns
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}
such that for all

i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, one of the following holds:

(i)

\theta_{i}
is an axiom.

(ii)

\theta_{i}\in\Gamma
.

(iii)

\theta_{i}
is the conclusion of an inference rule whose premise(s) are previous pattern(s).

An
\emptyset
-proof is called simply a
proof
.

Definition 4.10
.

Let
\varphi
be a pattern. A
\Gamma
-proof of
\varphi
or a

proof of
\varphi
from the hypotheses
\Gamma

is a
\Gamma
-proof

~\theta_{1}
,
\ldots
,
\theta_{n}
such that
\theta_{n}=\varphi
.

Proposition 4.11
.

For any pattern
\varphi
,

\Gamma\vdash\varphi
iff  there exists a
\Gamma
-proof of
\varphi
.

Proof.

Let us denote
\Theta=\{\varphi\in Pattern\mid\text{there exists a~}\Gamma\text{-proof of~}\varphi\}
.

\Rightarrow
We prove by induction on
\Gamma
-theorems that
Thm(\Gamma)\subseteq\Theta
:

(i)

\varphi
is an axiom or a member of
\Gamma
. Then
\varphi
is a
\Gamma
-proof of
\varphi
. Hence,
\varphi\in\Theta
.

(ii)

Let
\displaystyle\frac{\psi}{\varphi}
be an inference rule such that
\psi\in\Theta
. Then there exists a
\Gamma
-proof

\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}=\psi
of
\psi
. It follows that
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}=\psi,\theta_{n+1}=\varphi
is a

\Gamma
-proof of
\varphi
. Thus,
\varphi\in\Theta
.

(iii)

Let
\displaystyle\frac{\psi\quad\chi}{\varphi}
be an inference rule such that
\psi,\chi\in\Theta
.
Then there exist a
\Gamma
-proof
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}=\psi
of
\psi
and a
\Gamma
-proof

\delta_{1},\ldots,\delta_{p}=\chi
of
\chi
. It follows that

\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}=\psi,\theta_{n+1}=\delta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n+p}=\delta_{p}=\chi,\theta_{n+p+1}=\varphi
is a
\Gamma
-proof of
\varphi
. Thus,
\varphi\in\Theta
.

\Leftarrow
Assume that
\varphi
has a
\Gamma
-proof
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{n}=\varphi
. We prove by induction on
i
that for all

i=1,\ldots,n
,
\Gamma\vdash\theta_{i}
. As a consequence,
\Gamma\vdash\theta_{n}=\varphi
.

(i)

i=1
. Then
\theta_{1}
must be an axiom or a member of
\Gamma
. Then obviously
\Gamma\vdash\theta_{1}
.

(ii)

Assume that the induction hypothesis is true for all
j=1,\ldots,i
. If

\theta_{i+1}
is an axiom or a member of
\Gamma
, then obviously
\Gamma\vdash\theta_{i+1}
. Assume that
\theta_{i+1}
is the conclusion of
an inference rule whose premise(s) are previous pattern(s). If the inference rule is
of type
\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{j}}{\theta_{i+1}}
with
j\leq i
, then by the induction hypothesis we have that
\Gamma\vdash\theta_{j}
.
By the definition of
\Gamma
-theorems, it follows that
\Gamma\vdash\theta_{i+1}
.
If the inference rule is
of type
\displaystyle\frac{\theta_{j}\quad\theta_{k}}{\theta_{i+1}}
with
j,k\leq i
, then by the induction hypothesis we have that
\Gamma\vdash\theta_{j}
and

\Gamma\vdash\theta_{k}
.
By the definition of
\Gamma
-theorems, it follows that
\Gamma\vdash\theta_{i+1}
.

∎

4.2
Derived rules

In the sequel we present different rules that will help us in proving
\Gamma
-theorems.
These rules are of the form

If
A_{1}
,
A_{2}
, …,
A_{n}
(
n\geq 1
) hold, then
B
holds.

and will be written as
\displaystyle\frac{A_{1}\quad A_{2}\quad\ldots\quad A_{n}}{B}
.

Let
\Gamma
be a set of patterns.

Proposition 4.12
.

For every patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,

\displaystyle{\Gamma\vdash\varphi\quad}\mathstrut
\displaystyle\hskip 8.00003pt{\vDash^{t}\!\!\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi}\mathstrut

\displaystyle\Gamma\vdash\psi\mathstrut

(123)

Proof.

(1)

\Gamma

\vdash\varphi

hypothesis

(2)

\vdash\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi

(tautology)
: hypothesis

(3)

\vdash(\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi)\to(\varphi\to\psi)

(tautology)
: (
12
)

(4)

\vdash\varphi\to\psi

(modus ponens)
: (2), (3)

(5)

\Gamma

\vdash\varphi\to\psi

Proposition
4.8
.(
ii
)

(6)

\Gamma

\vdash\psi

(modus ponens)
: (1), (5).

∎

5
Soundness Theorem

5.1
\vDash_{s}

We consider the proof system

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\vDash_{s}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}-\{\textsc{($\exists$-quantifier rule)},\textsc{(framing)},\textsc{(set variable substitution)},\textsc{(knaster-tarski)}\}.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
be a set of patterns. Then

\Gamma\vdash\varphi
implies
\Gamma\vDash_{s}\varphi
.

Proof.

Let us denote
\Theta=\{\varphi\in Pattern\mid\Gamma\vDash_{s}\varphi\}
. We prove that
Thm(\Gamma)\subseteq\Theta

by induction on
\Gamma
-theorems.

Axioms:

(Tautology)
: By Proposition
3.66
and Remark
3.46
.(
ii
).

(
\exists
-Quantifier)
: By (
22
) and Remark
3.46
.(
ii
).

(Propagation
⊥
)
: By (
56
), (
57
) and Remark
3.46
.(
ii
).

(Propagation
∨
)
: By (
58
), (
59
) and Remark
3.46
.(
ii
).

(Propagation
∃
)
: By (
60
), (
61
) and Remark
3.46
.(
ii
).

(Pre-Fixpoint)
: By (
122
) and Remark
3.46
.(
ii
).

(Existence)
: By (
36
) and Remark
3.46
.(
ii
).

(Singleton Variable)
: By (
120
) and Remark
3.46
.(
ii
).

Rules:

(modus ponens)
: Assume that
\varphi,\varphi\to\psi\in\Theta
, hence
\Gamma\vDash_{s}\varphi
and
\Gamma\vDash_{s}\varphi\to\psi
. Apply (
41
) to get that

\Gamma\vDash_{s}\psi
, hence
\psi\in\Theta
.
∎

5.2
\vDash_{l}

We consider the proof system

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\vDash_{l}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{P}-\{\textsc{($\exists$-quantifier rule)},\textsc{(set variable substitution)}\}.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
be a set of patterns. Then

\Gamma\vdash\varphi
implies
\Gamma\vDash_{l}\varphi
.

Proof.

Let us denote
\Theta=\{\varphi\in Pattern\mid\Gamma\vDash_{l}\varphi\}
. We prove that
Thm(\Gamma)\subseteq\Theta

by induction on
\Gamma
-theorems.

Axioms:

If
\varphi
is an axiom, we have by Theorem
5.1
that
\Gamma\vDash_{s}\varphi
.
Apply now Proposition
3.52
to get that
\Gamma\vDash_{l}\varphi
, hence
\varphi\in\Theta
.

Rules:

(modus ponens)
: Assume that
\varphi,\psi\in\Theta
, hence
\Gamma\vDash_{l}\varphi
and
\Gamma\vDash_{l}\varphi\to\psi
. Apply (
45
) to get that

\Gamma\vDash_{l}\psi
, hence
\psi\in\Theta
.

(framing)
: Assume that
\varphi\to\psi\in\Theta
, hence
\Gamma\vDash_{l}\varphi\to\psi
. Apply (
74
) to get that

\Gamma\vDash_{l}{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi}
, hence
{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi}\in\Theta
, and (
76
) to get that

\Gamma\vDash_{l}{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\psi}
, hence
{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\psi}\in\Theta
.

(Knaster-Tarski)
: Assume that
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi
and that
{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\to\psi\in\Theta
,
hence
\Gamma\vDash_{l}{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\to\psi
. Apply Proposition
3.114

to get that
\Gamma\vDash_{l}\mu X.\varphi\to\psi
,
hence
\mu X.\varphi\to\psi\in\Theta
.
∎

5.3
\vDash_{g}

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}
be a set of patterns. Then

\Gamma\vdash\varphi
implies
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi
.

Proof.

The proof is by induction on
\Gamma
-theorems.

Axioms:

If
\varphi
is an axiom, we have by Theorem
5.1
that
\Gamma\vDash_{s}\varphi
.
Apply now Proposition
3.52
to get that
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi
, hence
\varphi\in\Theta
.

Rules:

(modus ponens)
: Assume that
\varphi,\psi\in\Theta
, hence
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi
and
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi\to\psi
. Apply (
49
) to get that

\Gamma\vDash_{g}\psi
, hence
\psi\in\Theta
.

(
\exists
-quantifier rule)
: Assume that
x\not\in FV(\psi)
and
\varphi\to\psi\in\Theta
, hence
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi\to\psi
.
Apply (
54
) to get that
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\exists x.\varphi\to\psi
, hence
\exists x.\varphi\to\psi\in\Theta
.

(framing)
: Assume that
\varphi\to\psi\in\Theta
, hence
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi\to\psi
. Apply (
94
) to get that

\Gamma\vDash_{g}{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi}
, hence
{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi}\in\Theta
, and (
96
) to get that

\Gamma\vDash_{g}{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\psi}
, hence
{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\psi}\in\Theta
.

(Set Variable Substitution)
: Assume that
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi
and
\varphi\in\Theta
, hence
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\varphi
.
Apply Proposition
3.111
to get that
\Gamma\vDash_{g}{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}
, hence
{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\in\Theta
.

(Knaster-Tarski)
: Assume that
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi
and that
{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\to\psi\in\Theta
,
hence
\Gamma\vDash_{g}{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\to\psi
. Apply Proposition
3.116

to get that
\Gamma\vDash_{g}\mu X.\varphi\to\psi
,
hence
\mu X.\varphi\to\psi\in\Theta
.

∎

6
Adding definedness, equality, totality, membership

In the sequel, we consider the signature
\tau_{DEF}=(EVar,SVar,\Sigma)
satisfying the following:

DEF\in\Sigma
;
DEF
is called the
definedness symbol
.

We introduce the derived connective
\left\lceil\cdot\right\rceil
,
\left\lfloor\cdot\right\rfloor
,
\mathbf{=}
by the following abbreviations:

\displaystyle\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil

\displaystyle:={DEF}\cdot{\varphi},

(124)

\displaystyle\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor

\displaystyle:=\neg\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil,

(125)

\displaystyle\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi

\displaystyle:=\left\lfloor\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi\right\rfloor,

(126)

\displaystyle x\in\varphi

\displaystyle:=\left\lceil x\wedge\varphi\right\rceil.

(127)

We assume that
\mathbf{=}
has higher precedence than
\wedge,\vee
,
\rightarrow
,
\leftrightarrow
.

Definition 6.1
.

A
\tau_{DEF}
-structure
\mathcal{A}
is obtained by defining
DEF^{\mathcal{A}}\subseteq A
such that

DEF^{\mathcal{A}}\star\{a\}=A\quad\text{for all }a\in A.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\tau_{DEF}
-structure. Then

(i)

A\star\{a\}=A
.

(ii)

DEF^{\mathcal{A}}\star B=A
for all
\emptyset\neq B\subseteq A
.

Proof.

(i)

We have that, for all
a\in A
,

A=DEF^{\mathcal{A}}\star\{a\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-se-C-appls-D}}}{{\subseteq}}A\star\{a\}\subseteq A.

Thus,
A\star\{a\}=A
.

(ii)

Let
\emptyset\neq B\subseteq A
. There exists
a\in B
. Then

A=DEF^{\mathcal{A}}\star\{a\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{B-se-C-appls-D}}}{{\subseteq}}DEF^{\mathcal{A}}\star B\subseteq A.

Thus,
DEF^{\mathcal{A}}\star B=A
.

∎

In the sequel, we write “Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
” instead of “Let
\mathcal{A}
be a
\tau_{DEF}
-structure
and
e
be an
\mathcal{A}
-valuation”.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
. For any pattern
\varphi
,

\displaystyle e^{+}(\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\text{if }e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset,\\
A&\text{if }e^{+}(\varphi)\neq\emptyset,\end{cases}

(128)

\displaystyle e^{+}(\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}A&\text{if }e^{+}(\varphi)=A,\\
\emptyset&\text{if }e^{+}(\varphi)\neq A.\end{cases}

(129)

Proof.

We get that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil)

\displaystyle=e^{+}({DEF}\cdot{\varphi})=e^{+}(DEF)\star e^{+}(\varphi)=DEF^{\mathcal{A}}\star e^{+}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\emptyset&\text{if }e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset,\quad\text{by \eqref{B-appl-empty}}\\
A&\text{if }e^{+}(\varphi)\neq\emptyset,\quad\text{by Lemma \ref{basic-prop-AMLsigdef}.\eqref{basic-prop-AMLsigdef-2}}\end{cases}

\displaystyle e^{+}(\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor)

\displaystyle=e^{+}(\neg\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil)=A\setminus e^{+}(\left\lceil\neg\varphi\right\rceil)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{evalp-ceil-vp}}}{{=}}\begin{cases}A\setminus\emptyset&\text{if }e^{+}(\neg\varphi)=\emptyset\\
A\setminus A&\text{if }e^{+}(\neg\varphi)\neq\emptyset\end{cases}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}A&\text{if }e^{+}(\neg\varphi)=\emptyset\\
\emptyset&\text{if }e^{+}(\neg\varphi)\neq\emptyset\end{cases}=\begin{cases}A&\text{if }A\setminus e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset\\
\emptyset&\text{if }A\setminus e^{+}(\varphi)\neq\emptyset\end{cases}=\begin{cases}A&\text{if }e^{+}(\varphi)=A\\
\emptyset&\text{if }e^{+}(\varphi)\neq A\end{cases}

∎

As immediate consequences of Proposition
6.3
, we get that

Proposition 6.4
.

For any pattern
\varphi
,
\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil
and
\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor
are predicate patterns.

Proposition 6.5
.

For any patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,
\varphi\mathbf{=}\psi
is a predicate pattern.

Proof.

Apply Proposition
6.4
and the definition of
\mathbf{=}
.
∎

Proposition 6.6
.

For any element variable
x
,

\vDash\left\lceil x\right\rceil.

(130)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
. As
e^{+}(x)\neq\emptyset
, we get that
e^{+}(\left\lceil x\right\rceil)=A
.
∎

Proposition 6.7
.

For any pattern
\varphi
,

\displaystyle\vDash

\displaystyle\varphi\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil,

(131)

\displaystyle\vDash

\displaystyle\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\to\varphi.

(132)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\vDash(\varphi\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil)[e]

iff

\displaystyle e^{+}(\varphi)\subseteq e^{+}(\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil),\text{ which is true, by \eqref{evalp-ceil-vp}},

(133)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\vDash(\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor\to\varphi)[e]

iff

\displaystyle e^{+}(\left\lfloor\varphi\right\rfloor)\subseteq e^{+}(\varphi),\text{ which is true, by \eqref{evalp-floor-vp}}.

(134)

∎

Proposition 6.8
.

For any distinct variables
x
,
y
,

\displaystyle\vDash\exists x.(x\mathbf{=}y)

(135)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
and
b:=e(y)
. Then

e^{+}(\exists x.(x\mathbf{=}y))=\bigcup_{a\in A}\left(e_{x\mapsto a}\right)^{+}(x\mathbf{=}y)\supseteq\left(e_{x\mapsto b}\right)^{+}(x\mathbf{=}y).

As

\left(e_{x\mapsto b}\right)^{+}(x\leftrightarrow y)=A\setminus(\left(e_{x\mapsto b}\right)^{+}(x)\Delta\left(e_{x\mapsto b}\right)^{+}(y))=A\setminus(\{b\}\Delta\{b\})=A,

we can apply (
129
) to get that

\left(e_{x\mapsto b}\right)^{+}(x\mathbf{=}y)=\left(e_{x\mapsto b}\right)^{+}(\left\lfloor x\leftrightarrow y\right\rfloor)=A
.

It follows that
e^{+}(\exists x(x\mathbf{=}y))=A
, that is
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\exists x(x\mathbf{=}y))[e]
.
∎

Proposition 6.9
.

For any patterns
\varphi
,
\psi
,

\displaystyle{\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil}\cdot{\psi}\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil,

(136)

\displaystyle{\psi}\cdot{\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil}\to\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil.

(137)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
. We have the following cases:

(i)

e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset
, hence
e^{+}(\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil)=\emptyset
.
Then
e^{+}({\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil}\cdot{\psi})=e^{+}(\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil)\star e^{+}(\psi)=\emptyset\star e^{+}(\psi)=\emptyset
and

e^{+}({\psi}\cdot{\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil})=e^{+}(\psi)\star e^{+}(\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil)=e^{+}(\psi)\star\emptyset=\emptyset
. Thus,
(
136
), (
137
) hold.

(ii)

e^{+}(\varphi)\neq\emptyset
, hence
e^{+}(\left\lceil\varphi\right\rceil)=A
. Then obviously
(
136
), (
137
) hold.

∎

Proposition 6.10
.

\displaystyle\vDash\left\lceil\bot\right\rceil\leftrightarrow\bot.

(138)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
. We have that
e^{+}(\left\lceil\bot\right\rceil)=\emptyset
, as
e^{+}(\bot)=\emptyset
.
∎

Proposition 6.11
.

For any pattern
\varphi
and variable
x
,

\displaystyle\vDash\neg(x\in\varphi)\vee\neg(x\in\neg\varphi).

(139)

Proof.

Let
(\mathcal{A},e)
.
Denote

\theta=\neg(x\in\varphi)\vee\neg(x\in\neg\varphi)=\neg\left\lceil x\wedge\varphi\right\rceil\vee\neg\left\lceil x\wedge\neg\varphi\right\rceil.

We have to prove that
\mathcal{A}\vDash(\theta)[e]
, that is,
e^{+}(\theta)=A
.

Remark that

\displaystyle e^{+}(\theta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle e^{+}(\neg\left\lceil x\wedge\varphi\right\rceil\vee\neg\left\lceil x\wedge\neg\varphi\right\rceil)=e^{+}(\neg\left\lceil x\wedge\varphi\right\rceil)\cup e^{+}(\neg\left\lceil x\wedge\neg\varphi\right\rceil)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{A}e^{+}(\left\lceil x\wedge\varphi\right\rceil)\cup C_{A}e^{+}(\left\lceil x\wedge\neg\varphi\right\rceil)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{de-morgan-2}}}{{=}}C_{A}\big(e^{+}(\left\lceil x\wedge\varphi\right\rceil)\cap e^{+}(\left\lceil x\wedge\neg\varphi\right\rceil)\big)

Thus,
e^{+}(\theta)=A
iff
e^{+}(\left\lceil x\wedge\varphi\right\rceil)\cap e^{+}(\left\lceil x\wedge\neg\varphi\right\rceil)=\emptyset
.

We have two cases:

(i)

e(x)\in e^{+}(\varphi)
. Then

e^{+}(x\wedge\neg\varphi)=e^{+}(x)\cap e^{+}(\neg\varphi)=\{e(x)\}\cap C_{A}e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset
. Hence, by (
128
),

e^{+}(\left\lceil x\wedge\neg\varphi\right\rceil)=\emptyset
. In particular,
e^{+}(\theta)=A
.

(ii)

e(x)\notin e^{+}(\varphi)
. Then

e^{+}(x\wedge\varphi)=e^{+}(x)\cap e^{+}(\varphi)=\{e(x)\}\cap e^{+}(\varphi)=\emptyset
. Hence, by (
128
),

e^{+}(\left\lceil x\wedge\varphi\right\rceil)=\emptyset
. In particular,
e^{+}(\theta)=A
.

∎

Appendix A
AML proof system
\mathcal{P}

(Tautology)

\varphi
if
\varphi
is a tautology

(
\exists
-Quantifier)

Subf^{x}_{y}{\varphi}\to\exists x.\varphi
if
x
is free
for
y
in
\varphi

(modus ponens)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi\quad\varphi\to\psi}{\psi}

(
\exists
-quantifier rule)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi\to\psi}{\exists x.\varphi\to\psi}
if
x\not\in FV(\psi)

(Propagation
⊥
)

{\varphi}\cdot{\bot}\to\bot

{\bot}\cdot{\varphi}\to\bot

(Propagation
∨
)

{(\varphi\vee\psi)}\cdot{\chi}\to{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\vee{\psi}\cdot{\chi}

{\chi}\cdot{(\varphi\vee\psi)}\to{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\vee{\chi}\cdot{\psi}

(Propagation
∃
)

{(\exists x.\varphi)}\cdot{\psi}\to\exists x.{\varphi}\cdot{\psi}

{\psi}\cdot{(\exists x.\varphi)}\to{\exists x.\psi}\cdot{\varphi}
if
x\not\in FV(\psi)

(framing)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi\to\psi}{{\varphi}\cdot{\chi}\to{\psi}\cdot{\chi}}\qquad\frac{\varphi\to\psi}{{\chi}\cdot{\varphi}\to{\chi}\cdot{\psi}}

(Set Variable Substitution)

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi}{{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}}
if
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi

(Pre-Fixpoint)

{Subf^{X}_{\mu X.\varphi}{\varphi}}\to\mu X.\varphi
if
\varphi
is positive in
X
and
X
is free for
\mu X.\varphi
in
\varphi

(Knaster-Tarski)

\displaystyle\frac{{Subf^{X}_{\psi}{\varphi}}\to\psi}{\mu X.\varphi\to\psi}
if
X
is free for
\psi
in
\varphi

(Existence)

\exists x.x

(Singleton Variable)

\neg(C_{1}[x\wedge\varphi]\wedge C_{2}[x\wedge\neg\varphi])

Appendix B
Set theory

Let
A
,
B
be sets. We use the following notations:

(i)

A\cup B
for the union of
A
and
B
.

(ii)

A\cap B
for the intersection of
A
and
B
.

(iii)

A\setminus B
for the difference between
A
and
B
.

(iv)

A\Delta B
for the symmetric difference of
A
and
B
.

(v)

2^{A}
for the powerset of
A
.

(vi)

C_{A}B
for the complementary of
B
, when
B\subseteq A
.

(vii)

Fun(A,B)
for the set of functions from
A
to
B
.

(viii)

A^{n}
for the set
A\times A\times\ldots\times A
, where
n\geq 2
.

Let
f:A\to B
be a mapping. For every
C\subseteq A
, we denote by
f|_{C}
the restriction of
f
to
C
. Thus,

f|_{C}:C\to B,\,(f|_{C})(x)=f(x)
for every
x\in C
.

B.1
Expressions over a set

An
expression over

A
is a finite sequence of elements from
A
. We denote an expression over
A
of
length
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
by
a_{1}a_{2}\ldots a_{n}
, where
a_{i}\in A
for every
i=1,\ldots,n
. The empty expression (of length
0
)
is denoted by
\lambda
. The concatenation of exoressions over
A
is defined as follows:
if
a=a_{1}\ldots a_{n}
and
b=b_{1}\ldots b_{k}
, then
ab=a_{1}\ldots a_{n}b_{1}\ldots b_{k}
.

Let
a=a_{0}a_{1}\ldots a_{n}
be an expression over
A
, where
a_{i}\in A

for all
i=0,\ldots,k-1
.

(i)

If
0\leq i\leq j\leq k-1
, then the expression
a_{i}\ldots a_{j}
is called
the
(i,j)
-
subexpression
of
a
.

(ii)

We say that an expression
b

occurs
in
a
if there exists

0\leq i\leq j\leq k-1
such that
b
is the
(i,j)
-subexpression of
a
.

(iii)

A
proper initial segment
of
a
is an expression
a_{0}a_{1}\ldots a_{i}
, where

0\leq i<n-1
.

B.2
Set-theoretic properties used in the lecture notes

Proposition B.1
.

Let
A,B,C,D
be sets. Then

\displaystyle B\subseteq C\text{~and~}B\subseteq D

\displaystyle\text{~implies~}B\subseteq C\cap D

(140)

\displaystyle A\setminus B=\emptyset

\displaystyle\text{~iff~}A\subseteq B

(141)

\displaystyle A\cup(B\cup C)=(A\cup B)\cup C,

(142)

\displaystyle A\cup(B\cap C)

\displaystyle=(A\cup B)\cap(A\cup C),

(143)

\displaystyle A\cap(B\cup C)

\displaystyle=(A\cap B)\cup(A\cap C),

(144)

\displaystyle(A\cap D)\cup(B\cap C)

\displaystyle=(A\cup B)\cap(A\cup C)\cap(D\cup B)\cap(D\cup C),

(145)

\displaystyle(A\cup D)\cap(B\cup C)

\displaystyle=(A\cap B)\cup(B\cap C)\cup(D\cap B)\cup(D\cap C),

(146)

\displaystyle A\Delta B

\displaystyle=\emptyset\quad\text{iff}\quad A=B,

(147)

\displaystyle A\setminus C

\displaystyle\subseteq(A\setminus B)\cup(B\setminus C)

(148)

Proposition B.2
.

Let
B,D\subseteq A
. Then

\displaystyle B\cup C_{A}B

\displaystyle=A,

(149)

\displaystyle C_{A}(C_{A}B)

\displaystyle=B

(150)

\displaystyle C_{A}(B\cup D)

\displaystyle=C_{A}B\cap C_{A}D

(151)

\displaystyle C_{A}(B\cap D)

\displaystyle=C_{A}B\cup C_{A}D

(152)

\displaystyle C_{A}(C_{A}B\cup C_{A}D)

\displaystyle=B\cap D

(153)

\displaystyle B\setminus D

\displaystyle=B\cap C_{A}D,

(154)

\displaystyle C_{A}(B\setminus D)

\displaystyle=C_{A}B\cup D

(155)

\displaystyle B\subseteq D

\displaystyle\text{~iff~}C_{A}D\subseteq C_{A}B

(156)

\displaystyle C_{A}B=\emptyset

\displaystyle\text{~iff~}B=A

(157)

\displaystyle C_{A}B=A

\displaystyle\text{~iff~}B=\emptyset,

(158)

\displaystyle C_{A}(B\setminus D)\cap C_{A}(D\setminus B)

\displaystyle=C_{A}(B\Delta D)

(159)

B.3
Families of sets

Let
(B_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of subsets of
A
.

Remark B.3
.

If
I=\emptyset
, then
\bigcap_{i\in I}B_{i}=A
.

Proposition B.4
.

\displaystyle C_{A}\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}B_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i\in I}C_{A}B_{i}

(160)

\displaystyle C_{A}\left(\bigcap_{i\in I}B_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in I}C_{A}B_{i}

(161)

\displaystyle A\cup\bigcap_{i\in I}B_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i\in I}(A\cup B_{i})

(162)

B.4
Application

In the sequel,
A
is a nonempty set and
\_\star\_:A\times A\to 2^{A}
is a binary
application
function.

We extend the application function
\_\star\_
as follows:

\_\star\_:2^{A}\times 2^{A}\to 2^{A},\quad B\star C=\bigcup_{b\in B,c\in C}b\star c.

Proposition B.5
.

Let
B,C,D\subseteq A
and
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
be a family of subsets of
A
.

\displaystyle B\star\emptyset

\displaystyle=\emptyset\star B=\emptyset,

(163)

\displaystyle(C\cup D)\star B

\displaystyle=(C\star B)\cup(D\star B),

(164)

\displaystyle B\star(C\cup D)

\displaystyle=(B\star C)\cup(B\star D),

(165)

\displaystyle(C\cap D)\star B

\displaystyle\subseteq(C\star B)\cap(D\star B),

(166)

\displaystyle B\star(C\cap D)

\displaystyle\subseteq(B\star C)\cap(B\star D),

(167)

\displaystyle\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)\star B

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in I}(A_{i}\star B),

(168)

\displaystyle B\star\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in I}(B\star A_{i}),

(169)

\displaystyle\left(\bigcap_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)\star B

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}(A_{i}\star B),

(170)

\displaystyle B\star\left(\bigcap_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcap_{i\in I}(B\star A_{i}),

(171)

\displaystyle B\subseteq C

\displaystyle\text{~implies~}B\star D\subseteq C\star D\text{~and~}D\star B\subseteq D\star C

(172)

\displaystyle B\subseteq C\text{~and~}D\subseteq E

\displaystyle\text{~implies~}B\star D\subseteq C\star E

(173)

\displaystyle B\subseteq C

\displaystyle\text{~implies~}C_{A}B\star D\supseteq C_{A}C\star D\text{~and~}D\star C_{A}B\supseteq D\star C_{A}C

(174)

Proof.

(
163
): Obviously.

(
164
):
(C\cup D)\star B=\bigcup_{c\in C\cup D,b\in B}c\star b=\left(\bigcup_{c\in C,b\in B}c\star b\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{c\in D,b\in B}c\star b\right)=(C\star B)\cup(D\star B)
.

(
165
):
B\star(C\cup D)=\bigcup_{b\in B,c\in C\cup D}b\star c=\left(\bigcup_{b\in B,c\in C}b\star c\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{b\in B,c\in D}b\star c\right)=(B\star C)\cup(B\star D)
.

(
166
): Let
a\in(C\cap D)\star B
. Then
a=e\star b
with
e\in C\cap D
and
b\in B
.
It follows that
a\in(C\star B)\cap(D\star B)
.

(
167
): Let
a\in B\star(C\cap D)
. Then
a=b\star e
with
e\in C\cap D
and
b\in B
.
It follows that

a\in(B\star C)\cap(B\star D)
.

(
168
):
\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)\star B=\bigcup_{a\in\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i},b\in B}a\star b=\bigcup_{i\in I}\bigcup_{a\in A_{i},b\in B}a\star b=\bigcup_{i\in I}(A_{i}\star B)
.

(
169
):
B\star\left(\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)=\bigcup_{b\in B,a\in\bigcup_{i\in I}A_{i}}b\star a=\bigcup_{i\in I}\bigcup_{a\in A_{i},b\in B}b\star a=\bigcup_{i\in I}(B\star A_{i})
.

(
170
): Let
a\in\left(\bigcap_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)\star B
. Then
a=e\star b
with
b\in B
and
e\in\bigcap_{i\in I}A_{i}
,
hence
a\in A_{i}
for all
i\in I
. It follows that
a\in\bigcap_{i\in I}(A_{i}\star B)
.

(
171
): Let
a\in B\star\left(\bigcap_{i\in I}A_{i}\right)
. Then
a=b\star e
with
b\in B
and
e\in\bigcap_{i\in I}A_{i}
,
hence
a\in A_{i}
for all
i\in I
. It follows that
a\in\bigcap_{i\in I}(B\star A_{i})
.

(
172
):
B\star D=\bigcup_{b\in B,d\in D}b\star d\subseteq\bigcup_{b\in C,d\in D}b\star d=C\star D
and, similarly,
D\star B\subseteq D\star C
.

(
173
): Apply (
172
) twice to get that
B\star D\subseteq C\star D\subseteq C\star E
.

(
174
) As
B\subseteq C
, we have that
C_{A}B\supseteq C_{A}C
. Apply (
172
).
∎

B.5
Knaster-Tarski Theorem

In the sequel
A
is a nonempty set and
F:2^{A}\to 2^{A}
is a mapping.

Definition B.6
.

A subset
D
of
A
is a
fixpoint
of
F
if
F(D)=D
.

Definition B.7
.

A subset
D
of
A
is

(i)

the least fixpoint
of
F
if
D
is a fixpoint of
F
and for every fixpoint

D^{\prime}
of
F
, we have that
D\subseteq D^{\prime}
.

(ii)

the greatest fixpoint
of
F
if
D
is a fixpoint of
F
and for every fixpoint

D^{\prime}
of
F
, we have that
D\supseteq D^{\prime}
.

Definition B.8
.

F
is said to be
monotone
if for every
B,C\subseteq A
,

B\subseteq C\quad\text{implies}\quad F(B)\subseteq F(C).

Theorem B.9

(Knaster-Tarski
[
9
]
)
.

Let
F:2^{A}\to 2^{A}
be a monotone function. Define

\displaystyle\mu F=\bigcap\{B\subseteq A\mid F(B)\subseteq B\}\quad\text{and}\quad\nu F=\bigcup\{C\subseteq A\mid C\subseteq F(C)\}.

(175)

Then
\mu F
is the least fixpoint of
F
and
\nu F
is the greatest fixpoint of
F
. Furthermore,

\displaystyle\mu F=\bigcap\{D\subseteq A\mid F(D)=D\}\quad\text{and}\quad\nu F=\bigcup\{D\subseteq A\mid F(D)=D\}.

(176)

Proof.

Let us denote
\mathcal{B}=\{B\subseteq A\mid F(B)\subseteq B\}
and
\mathcal{C}=\{C\subseteq A\mid C\subseteq F(C)\}
.

Claim 1:

F(\mu F)=\mu F
.

Proof of claim:
“
\subseteq
” For every
B\in\mathcal{B}
, we have
that
\mu F\subseteq B
, hence
F(\mu F)\subseteq F(B)\subseteq B
.
It follows that
F(\mu F)\subseteq\bigcap\mathcal{B}=\mu F
.

”
\supseteq
” As
F(\mu F)\subseteq\mu F
, we have that
F(F(\mu F))\subseteq F(\mu F)
, so
F(\mu F)\in\mathcal{B}
.
It follows that
\mu F\subseteq F(\mu F)
.
\blacksquare

Claim 2:

\mu F
is the least fixpoint of
F
.

Proof of claim:
If
D
is another fixpoint of
F
, we have, in particular, that
F(D)\subseteq D
,
so
D\in\mathcal{B}
. It follows that
\mu F\subseteq D
.
\blacksquare

Claim 3:

F(\nu F)=\nu F
.

Proof of claim:
“
\supseteq
” For every
C\in\mathcal{C}
, we have that
C\subseteq\nu F
.
It follows that
F(C)\subseteq F(\nu F)
, so

C\subseteq F(C)\subseteq F(\nu F)
.
It follows that
\nu F=\bigcup\mathcal{C}\subseteq F(\nu F)
.

“
\subseteq
” As
\nu F\subseteq F(\nu F)
, we have that
F(\nu F)\subseteq F(F(\nu F))
, so
F(\nu F)\in\mathcal{C}
.
It follows that
F(\nu F)\subseteq\nu F
.
\blacksquare

Claim 4:

\nu F
is the greatest fixpoint of
F
.

Proof of claim:
If
D
is another fixpoint of
F
, we have, in particular, that
D\subseteq F(D)
,
so
D\in\mathcal{C}
. It follows that
D\subseteq\nu F
.
\blacksquare

Let us denote
\mathcal{D}=\{D\subseteq A\mid F(D)=D\}
.

Claim 5:

\mu F=\bigcap\mathcal{D}
.

Proof of claim:
“
\supseteq
” We have, by Claim 1, that
\mu F\in\mathcal{D}
, hence
\mu F\supseteq\bigcap\mathcal{D}
.

”
\subseteq
” For every
D\in\mathcal{D}
, we have, by Claim 2, that
\mu F\subseteq D
.
Hence,
\mu F\subseteq\bigcap\mathcal{D}
.
\blacksquare

Claim 6:

\nu F=\bigcup\mathcal{D}
.

Proof of claim:
“
\subseteq
” We have, by Claim 3, that
\nu F\in\mathcal{D}
, hence
\nu F\subseteq\bigcup\mathcal{D}
.

”
\supseteq
” For every
D\in\mathcal{D}
, we have, by Claim 4, that
D\subseteq\nu F
. Hence,
\bigcup\mathcal{D}\subseteq\nu F
.
∎

Thus, if
F:2^{A}\to 2^{A}
is monotone, then
F(\mu F)=\mu F
,
F(\nu F)=\nu F
and for every

B\subseteq A
such that
F(B)=B
,

\mu F\subseteq B\subseteq\nu F.
